  - Once built, checked on 2026-10-05 (OWL-157) through Owlshift's own code, `crates/owlshift-runner/tests/linear_app_live.rs`, run by hand on test issue OWL-168, which the key created and assigned to its own account and the test then deleted to Linear's trash with its comment. `tracker::linear` requested the app's token and accepted it (an app user of the key's workspace that sees team `OWL`). The Writer's QUESTIONS round read back through the tracker verbatim, marked, holding the decider's profile link, its author not an account and `user.app` true. The stage moved through the app. Of two more tokens with the same scopes, revoking one left the other answering HTTP 200, the revoked one 401, so a command's revocation leaves another command's token alone. The decider had one notification on the issue, the `issueCommentMention` of that comment from the app user; there was no assignment notification, since the issue was assigned through the decider's own key, of which Linear does not notify them. The tracker's token was revoked when it was dropped. The Linear fixtures were recorded again that day, read-only, for the comment query that reads `user.app`.
- Questions as clickable choices without a public webhook, checked on 2026-10-19 (OWL-354) on the Owlshift workspace with Owlshift's own Linear app and a client credentials token of scopes `read,write`, on a sandbox issue (OWL-370, Backlog, unassigned) that was deleted to Linear's trash at the end with its comment and agent session. Verdict (A): the choices are clickable and the answer comes back by polling, with no public endpoint; two qualifications follow below. D8 is unchanged, and nothing in Owlshift's code was built.
  - Schema, by introspection that day: `AgentSessionCreateOnIssue` has `issueId` (required), `externalLink` and `externalUrls`. `AgentActivityCreateInput` has `id`, `agentSessionId` (required), `signal`, `signalMetadata`, `contextualMetadata`, `content` (required, a JSON object) and `ephemeral`. `AgentActivityContent` is a union of `AgentActivityThoughtContent`, `AgentActivityActionContent`, `AgentActivityResponseContent`, `AgentActivityPromptContent` (`type`, `body`, `title`, `bodyData`), `AgentActivityErrorContent` and `AgentActivityElicitationContent`. `AgentSession` has a `status` and `activities`.
  - The app as D8 configures it, with no webhook: `agentSessionCreateOnIssue` on OWL-370 was refused, HTTP 400 `INPUT_ERROR`, "Agent sessions are not enabled for this application." No scope was named, so the refusal is about the app's configuration, not its token's scopes.
  - Then the maintainer turned webhooks on for the app, with a URL that does not answer (`https://example.com/owl354-hook`), and ticked the **Agent session events** category. No scope was added (`app:mentionable` and `app:assignable` were not requested), so no token was revoked. Linear's [agents guide](https://linear.app/developers/agents), read that day, names that webhook category as what makes an app an agent, and presents `app:mentionable`, `app:assignable` and an `actor=app` install as optional. So the first qualification: the choices need the Agent session events category on, which needs a webhook URL, though that URL need not answer. That departs from D8's "no webhook" if it is ever adopted. The webhook stayed enabled after the deliveries failed, as the app's settings showed afterwards, and whether Linear marked the session or the app as unresponsive was not noticed. The maintainer turns the webhook off again.
  - The question: with the same `read,write` token, `agentSessionCreateOnIssue` answered a session with `status` `active` and its own `/agent-session/<id>` URL. `agentActivityCreate` with `signal: "select"`, `signalMetadata: { options: [{label:"Approve",value:"approve"},{label:"Change it",value:"change"},{label:"Park it",value:"park"}] }` and `content: { type: "elicitation", body: "…" }` succeeded.
  - What the maintainer saw (screenshots taken by the maintainer, not committed): the options are not buttons in the issue's thread. They sit in the chat input bar of a separate agent-session panel that opens like a chat beside the issue, which the maintainer judged inconvenient. That is the second qualification.
  - The answer: the maintainer clicked "Approve". Polling `agentSession(id) { status activities { nodes { … } } }` every 5 s with the app token, with no webhook receiver, returned a new activity whose `content` is an `AgentActivityPromptContent` with `body` `"approve"`, the option's value and not its label; its `signal` and `signalMetadata` are null and its `user` is the maintainer (`app` false). It showed at the first poll after the click (created 46 s after the elicitation, the time the maintainer took), and the session's `status` became `pending`. The field that carries the choice is therefore that `PromptContent.body`.
  - Free text: to a second `select` elicitation in the same session, the maintainer typed text instead of clicking. It came back the same way, a `PromptContent` whose `body` is the typed text. Nothing else tells a click from free text, so a reader tells them apart by matching `body` against the option values.
  - Several questions: two elicitations in a row in one session both worked; the second appeared after the first in the session's conversation, the first answer staying visible. One session per question and combined choices were not tried.
  - Reactions, with D8's app as configured (before the webhook change): the app posted a question comment on OWL-370 and the maintainer added a thumbs-up. `comment(id) { reactions { emoji createdAt user { displayName app } } }`, read with the app token, returned `emoji` `"+1"` and the maintainer as `user` (`app` false) at the first 5-second poll after the reaction (reaction 20:07:35 UTC, seen 20:07:40); the personal key read the same. This route needs no change to D8. It was filed as OWL-371 and built there ("A thumbs-up answers a one-question round", below). No follow-up was filed for the agent-session route, by the maintainer's choice.
  - Every token was revoked at the end (`POST https://api.linear.app/oauth/revoke`, HTTP 200).
- Reactions as OWL-371 reads them, checked on 2026-10-19 between 21:24 and 21:25 UTC, a second check after OWL-354's thumbs-up of 20:07 UTC above, through the personal key by a one-off script that printed no key, name or identifier, so no fixture was recorded.
  - Read only, on OWL-160 (26 comments). `Reaction` has the fields `id`, `createdAt`, `updatedAt`, `archivedAt`, `emoji`, `issue`, `comment`, `projectUpdate`, `initiativeUpdate`, `post`, `user` and `externalUser`, and no `botActor`. `Comment.reactions` is a non-null list of `Reaction`, and `reactionData` is a `JSONObject`. A page of 50 comments costs 9 complexity points with today's query, and 12 with `reactions { emoji createdAt user { id displayName app } externalUser { name } }` nested in each comment.
  - Writes, on the newest comment of OWL-374, a canceled ticket (OWL-354 has none left), a comment posted earlier through the same key. `reactionCreate` with `+1` succeeded: `emoji` `+1`, its `user` the key's account by `id`, `app` false. `comment(id)` and `issue.comments` both listed it. The comment's `editedAt` was unchanged and its `updatedAt` changed. `reactionDelete` succeeded, and both reads then listed none.
  - So a reaction's account is read by its id, a removal shows at the next read, and a reaction does not read as an edit through `editedAt`. Nothing in Owlshift reads `updatedAt`.

**C6 — passed for registration.** The `.dev` registry's RDAP service (`https://pubapi.registry.google/rdap/domain/owlshift.dev`, where `rdap.org` redirects) answers 404 "owlshift.dev not found", while `google.dev` returns a domain record; `.dev` has no whois server. So the name is not registered. Whether a registrar prices it as premium or the registry reserves it shows only at checkout; the registrar search pages tried render availability in JavaScript only.

**C7 (Claude) — terms: ambiguous, leaning permitted. Usage limit: detectable.**

- Terms, read on 2026-09-28; a reading, not a legal conclusion. The [Consumer Terms](https://www.anthropic.com/legal/consumer-terms) (effective October 8, 2025), section 3 "Use of our Services", forbid accessing the Services "through automated or non-human means", except with an API key "or where we otherwise explicitly permit it". The Claude Code [legal and compliance page](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/legal-and-compliance) forbids third-party developers "to route requests through Free, Pro, or Max plan credentials on behalf of their users", but does not prevent a user "signing in to the unmodified Claude Code binary with their own Claude subscription"; it adds that advertised limits "assume ordinary, individual usage". The help-center article [15036540](https://support.claude.com/en/articles/15036540) (June 2026) says `claude -p` still draws "from your subscription's usage limits", so headless use on a subscription is an anticipated, metered use. Owlshift sits on the permitted side of these lines: the user's own login, the unmodified `claude` binary, no credential handled or routed by Owlshift. Residual risk: whether an unattended runner working through a queue still counts as "ordinary, individual usage", and the same article announces a change to how Agent SDK and `claude -p` usage is counted, paused on June 15, 2026. P0 proceeds; a change to either page reopens this check.
  - Note of 2026-10-02 (OWL-121): this reading predates the agent login of OWL-94 (2026-09-30, below), and its "no credential handled or routed by Owlshift" no longer holds as written. Owlshift now handles one credential: a long-lived token of the operator's own subscription. The operator makes it with `claude setup-token` and `owlshift init` stores it in the system keychain. The runner reads it and hands it to the unmodified `claude` binary on the same machine, on a pipe that the harness command alone inherits; the token itself is never in an environment (OWL-96). Owlshift sends the token nowhere else and makes no model request itself. No gateway or provider variable reaches an agent run (OWL-120, below), so the binary sends its requests to Anthropic's API, through the operator's proxy when one is set, unless the machine's managed settings route them elsewhere. The two other facts the reading relied on are unchanged: the login is of the operator's own subscription, and the binary is unmodified. Whether the terms read the same with Owlshift holding this token was not re-read: an open item of C7, beside the residual risk above.
- Usage limit: the [error reference](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/errors) documents the message `You've hit your session limit · resets 3:45pm` (also weekly, Opus and Sonnet limits). The [Agent SDK reference](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/typescript) documents a `rate_limit_event` whose `status` is `allowed`, `allowed_warning` or `rejected`, with `resetsAt` in epoch seconds, and an assistant error `rate_limit` for a 429 against the quota. Live: every `--output-format stream-json` run above emitted a `rate_limit_event` (`status: "allowed"`, `resetsAt`, `rateLimitType: "five_hour"`, utilisation of the five-hour and seven-day windows). Logged: headless sessions on this machine (entrypoint `sdk-cli`, Claude Code 2.1.251, 2026-08-29 and 30) recorded the limit as a synthetic assistant message with `error: "rate_limit"`, `apiErrorStatus: 429` and the text `You've hit your session limit · resets 2pm (<time zone>)`. The runner therefore reads the limit and its reset time from the stream, not from the message text.
- Open item: the exit status and the final `result` record of `claude -p` when a limit is reached were not observed; the subscription was not exhausted on purpose. OWL-14 and OWL-15 record them the first time a real run hits a limit. The failure case above (status 1, `is_error: true`, `api_error_status`) is the likely shape, not a verified one.

**C7 (Codex) — terms: automation on a ChatGPT login documented, an API key preferred. Usage limit: detectable, its reset time in the text only (OWL-297).**

- **Terms**, read on 2026-10-15. This is a reading, not a legal conclusion.
  - OpenAI's [Terms of Use](https://openai.com/policies/row-terms-of-use/) (effective January 1, 2026) were read from the Wayback Machine's capture of 2026-10-10, because openai.com answers 403 to clients that are not browsers. Under "What you cannot do" they forbid to "automatically or programmatically extract data or Output", and to circumvent rate limits. Under "Registration" they forbid sharing account credentials.
  - The terms for users residing in the EEA, which govern the maintainer, could not be fetched (403, then 429 from the archive).
  - Codex's own [Non-interactive mode](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/non-interactive-mode) page documents `codex exec` in scripts and CI. Its section "Use ChatGPT-managed auth in CI/CD (advanced)" is written for trusted runners and for users who need ChatGPT rate limits rather than API-key billing. The same section calls API keys "the right default for automation" and says not to use that workflow for public or open-source repositories; that warning is about a login stored on a CI runner.
  - Codex's [pricing](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/pricing) page includes Codex in the CLI with the ChatGPT plans: Plus has a five-hour window, and weekly limits may apply.
  - An Owlshift run is the user's own unmodified `codex`, on the user's own machine and login, which is the case these pages document.
- **Usage limit, live.** It was captured on 2026-10-15 by chance, when the subscription was at its limit as the first runs started (`usage_limit`).
  - The stream was `thread.started`, `turn.started`, then an `error` event whose `message` reads "You've hit your usage limit. Upgrade to Pro (…), visit … to purchase more credits or try again at 1:45 PM.", then `turn.failed` with the same message. The run exited 1, and standard error said nothing more.
  - The reset time is only in that text, as a wall-clock time of the local zone, with no date; no event field carries it. Without `--json`, the same text follows `ERROR:`.
  - Interactive Codex sessions on the same machine log `token_count` events carrying a `rate_limits` object: `primary` and `secondary` windows with `used_percent`, `window_minutes` and `resets_at` in epoch seconds, `plan_type` and `rate_limit_reached_type`. `codex exec --json` emitted none.
  - So the adapter recognises a limit by its message, `You've hit your usage limit`. The binary also holds `You're out of credits` and `Usage limit reached`.
  - *Wordings observed* (OWL-341). Each ends its text the same way, with a 12-hour time, an upper-case `AM` or `PM`, and a full stop; no date.
    - 2026-10-15, live call, recording `usage_limit`: "You've hit your usage limit. Upgrade to Pro (…), visit … to purchase more credits or try again at 1:45 PM."
    - 2026-10-18, from the ticket OWL-341, which quotes the message the design reviews of OWL-330, OWL-339 and LOC-1406 stopped on at 22:44–22:55 UTC: "You've hit your usage limit. … try again at 3:54 AM." The stored logs of those runs could not be read from the Build's sandbox, so the text between the two ellipses is as the ticket gives it. The time was 03:54 local, the next morning.
- **Open items.** The EEA terms are unread. The weekly limit's wording, a date-qualified reset, was not captured: no limit of that kind was reached, and a Build has no network to provoke one. `You're out of credits` and `Usage limit reached` are in the binary only, not observed, and carry no time; none of them is read as a reset time.

**OWL-14 — what the Claude Code adapter relies on, beyond C1 and C7.** Tiny `claude -p` runs on 2026-09-28 with Claude Code 2.1.283 on macOS, on `haiku` at effort `low`, in a scratch directory, each launched with `env -i HOME PATH USER LANG TMPDIR` and `--setting-sources project,local --strict-mcp-config --no-session-persistence --permission-prompts none`.

- `--output-format stream-json` needs `--verbose` with `-p`: without it the CLI exits 1 with `Error: When using --print, --output-format=stream-json requires --verbose`. The prompt can come on standard input, with no prompt argument; the adapter sends it there, which keeps it off the command line.
- The stream of a tiny run is five lines: `system/init`, two `assistant` messages, a `rate_limit_event`, `result`; standard error stays empty. `init` carries `claude_code_version`, `model` and `apiKeySource` (`none`). `result` carries `is_error`, `result` (the final text), `usage` (input, output, cache read and cache creation tokens), `total_cost_usd`, `duration_ms`, `num_turns`, `modelUsage` (the same counts per model, camelCase, with `costUSD`), `permission_denials` (with `tool_name`), `api_error_status` and `terminal_reason`.
- Read-only: `--permission-mode dontAsk --allowedTools 'Edit(./result.json)'` let the Write tool create `result.json` and denied a Write to `other.txt`, listed in `permission_denials`; C1 had already seen `dontAsk` deny Bash. Since OWL-323 (C1, above) the rule is anchored at the working directory, `Edit(/result.json)`: a `./` rule follows the shell's `cd`.
- Write in worktree: `--permission-mode acceptEdits --allowedTools Bash` let Write create a file in the working directory and Bash run `echo hi > bash.txt`, and denied a Write to `../outside.txt`. Bash itself is not confined to the directory.
- No network: `--disallowedTools WebFetch WebSearch` removes both tools from the `init` tool list; asked to fetch a page, the model answered that WebFetch is unavailable.
- Unknown model: besides C1's `result` record, the `assistant` message carries a top-level `error: "model_not_found"`, and standard error one line, `[claude-code:unrecognized_model] {…}`. The logged limit message of C7 has the same shape with `error: "rate_limit"`.
- Launched from inside a Claude Code desktop session, with its environment inherited (`CLAUDECODE`, `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, `CLAUDE_EFFORT` and others), the run still reported `apiKeySource: none` and added a `system/post_turn_summary` event. Which variables a run inherits is settled by OWL-22, below: none of these.

**OWL-22 — what an agent inherits.** Checked on 2026-09-28 on macOS with gh 2.91.0, git 2.54.0 (Apple Git-157), OpenSSH 10.3p1 and Claude Code 2.1.283, on the maintainer's machine: gh logged in through the system keyring, the `osxkeychain` helper in the git configuration, pushes to GitHub over SSH (C3). The only network calls made carried no credential.

- gh: pointing `GH_CONFIG_DIR` at an empty directory does not log gh out: `gh auth token` still returned the keyring login (exit 0), gh falling back to the host's unkeyed keyring entry. Rejected. On the same machine, `GH_TOKEN` set to a placeholder wins over the keyring login and over a token in gh's configuration file (`gh auth token` prints the placeholder); `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN` does the same for any other host; the API answers the placeholder with `Bad credentials (HTTP 401)`.
- git: with a helper that returns a password, plain or scoped to `https://github.com`, `git -c credential.helper= credential fill` returned none (`could not read Username … terminal prompts disabled`): an empty value in the command scope, read last, resets the helper list. With `core.askPass` naming a program that prints a password, an empty `GIT_ASKPASS` made git skip it, and `SSH_ASKPASS` too. The environment form of the command scope (`GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, git 2.31 or later) is exercised by the test below on the three CI platforms.
- SSH: `ssh -o BatchMode=yes -o IdentityAgent=none -o PubkeyAuthentication=no -o GSSAPIAuthentication=no` to GitHub as `git` answered `Permission denied (publickey)`.
- MCP: given one server with `--mcp-config` next to the guardrail flags, the `init` event listed `{"name":"owlshift-probe","status":"failed","source":"dynamic"}`: a server that failed to start is still listed. The recorded runs, flags alone, list none.

Decision: an agent process starts from an empty environment (`owlshift_core::agent_env`). It inherits what a program needs to run on the three platforms, Codex's login location (`CODEX_HOME`; a Claude Code run gets its configuration folder from the runner since OWL-94), the locale, the operator's proxy settings when they hold no login (OWL-76, below) and certificate settings, and the variables the project declares for its gate; a declared credential variable, `GIT_*` name, override, Claude Code or Codex variable (OWL-120, OWL-125, below) is refused. `GH_TOKEN` and `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN` then hold the placeholder `owlshift-agent-has-no-credential`, `credential.helper` is reset through `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, `GIT_ASKPASS` is empty, `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT` is 0, and `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` runs ssh with the options above. Everything else is dropped: token variables, `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, `GIT_*`, `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` and `DISPLAY` (they locate the Secret Service), and a parent Claude Code session's variables. `HOME` stays: redirecting it would hide the harness's own login too.

- **Declared variables (OWL-44).** A project names them in `owlshift.toml` under `stack.gate_env`, next to `stack.gate`: `gate_env = ["FEATURE_FLAGS"]`. Names only; each takes its value, as is, from the runner's environment, and one the runner lacks is not set. Under confinement, the folder a declared value names under the home is opened read-only by the rule of OWL-68 (`AgentEnv::policy`, below). `owlshift_core::agent_env::check_names` refuses, in any letter case, a name that is empty or holds `=` or NUL, a credential variable of the floor's list, a `GIT_*` name or an override, a dynamic-loader variable (`LD_*`, `DYLD_*`, `GCONV_PATH`, OWL-41, OWL-125), an interpreter's start-up code (`BASH_ENV`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, OWL-125), and a variable of Claude Code's own (`ANTHROPIC_*`, `CLAUDE_*`, `CLAUDECODE`, OWL-120) or of Codex's (`CODEX_*`, `OPENAI_*`, OWL-125), below. There is one agent environment per run, so the variables reach the harness and every command it starts, not only the gate. `owlshift init` writes the key as a commented example.
- **Operator allow-list (OWL-63).** Decision: a declared name reaches an agent only if the operator lists it in the personal file, `allow_gate_env = ["FEATURE_FLAGS"]`; a project's declaration never widens that list, and no personal file means no name passes. Rejected: refusing secret-shaped names by pattern (`*_TOKEN`, `*SECRET*`, `*PASSWORD*`, `*_API_KEY`). A pattern misses a secret under a plain name, such as a password inside `DATABASE_URL`, and catches names that hold none, such as `TOKENIZERS_PARALLELISM` under a substring match; a pattern with an operator override keeps the first gap. The project file is committed, so any contributor whose change is merged can edit it, while the personal file is the operator's alone; the agent environment is already built from a list of what passes (`INHERITED`), and declared variables were the one entry the repository controlled. The cost: each name, a harmless one included, takes one line in the personal file, and the refusal says so. `check_declared(declared, allowed)` applies `check_names` first, so the floor's names stay refused even when listed, then refuses every declared name the list lacks, in any letter case, naming each once: "DATABASE_URL may not reach an agent on this machine: the operator allows a name in the personal configuration, for every repository in `allow_gate_env`, or for one in the `allow_gate_env` of its `[repositories."github.com/<owner>/<name>"]` table". The personal file is invalid when `allow_gate_env` holds a name `check_names` refuses. The rule runs when the configuration is loaded: `ProjectConfig::validate` applies `check_names` alone, since the file cannot know the operator's list, and `Effective::load`, which holds both files, applies `ProjectConfig::check_gate_env`, so the project file is invalid with `stack.gate_env: ` and the reason, and `owlshift do` refuses to start; with the personal file itself invalid, the configuration is already invalid and the project is left unchecked. It runs again when the environment is built (`AgentEnv::for_project`, `AgentEnv::from_runner`). `allow_gate_env` is per machine: a name listed there passes for every project run on the machine that declares it; OWL-75, below, scopes a name to one repository. Known limits: names compare in any letter case, so on Unix, where `FEATURE_FLAGS` and `feature_flags` are two variables, both reach the agent once either is declared and allowed, as with the inherited variables; the inherited names, the locale and proxy ones included, are outside this list, each named in the code (OWL-76, below).
- **Repository scope (OWL-75, decided 2026-09-29).** Decision: the operator can allow a name for one repository only, in the personal file, next to the machine-wide list: `[repositories."github.com/acme/api"]` then `allow_gate_env = ["DATABASE_URL"]`. A project of that repository gets the machine-wide names and its own (`PersonalConfig::allow_gate_env_names`); a project of another gets the machine-wide names alone. The repository is its checkout's `origin` remote, read as `owlshift do` reads it (`project::origin_text`, `on_demand::check_origin`) and keyed `github.com/<owner>/<name>`, the path its data directory already takes (`ProjectDirs`); keys compare in any letter case, as GitHub names do. Why the remote: the key must come from something the committed project file does not control, or a project could name another repository and take its allowances. `origin` lives in the checkout's git configuration, which is not committed and is set by whoever cloned; agents run in worktrees of the runner's own clone (`ProjectDirs::checkout`), so an agent cannot rewrite the remote of the operator's checkout either. Rejected: a key the project file declares, such as a project name, spoofable by any merged change; the checkout's path, which breaks on a move or a second clone and is not how the runner names a project anywhere else. The remote is read with `Repo::from_github_remote`, which reads user info inside the URL's authority only: before OWL-75, an `@` in the path let `https://example.invalid/@github.com/acme/api.git` pass for `acme/api`, and it is now refused. When the configuration is loaded, `Effective::load` reads the remote only if the project declares a name, and checks the project against the names allowed for that repository; with no remote, a remote not on GitHub or git failing, the repository is unknown, only the machine-wide names apply, and the refusal says why ("names scoped to a repository do not apply: …"), or else names the repository it read ("this repository is github.com/acme/web"). `owlshift do` then builds the agent environment from the names allowed for the repository it delivers to (`Effective::allowed_gate_env(Some(&repo))`), which `AgentEnv::from_runner` checks again, so the load's reading can refuse early but never widen what a run gets. The personal file is invalid when a key is not `github.com/<owner>/<name>` (other forges are not delivered to yet) or when any entry's list holds a name `check_names` refuses. The scenario runner refuses a scenario whose personal file uses `repositories`, since its remote is a local bare repository. Known limit: the allowance follows the remote, not the content, so every checkout whose `origin` is `acme/api` gets it, whatever branch is checked out; the data directory, unlike the key, keeps the remote's letter case.
- **Setup and caches (OWL-330, decided 2026-10-19).** Next to `stack.gate` and `stack.gate_env`, a project names the commands that install its gate's dependencies, `stack.setup`, and the home folders they read, `stack.caches`; a cache is opened read-only, and only when the operator lists it in the personal file's `allow_caches`, for every repository or for one, as `allow_gate_env` is. Otherwise the project file is invalid at load, naming `allow_caches`; a credential path is refused listed or not. See "Preparing a gate's dependencies (OWL-330)", above.
- **Locale and proxy variables (OWL-76, decided 2026-09-29).** The locale stays: `LANG`, `LANGUAGE` and the thirteen POSIX and glibc categories, named in `LOCALE_CATEGORIES` (`LC_ALL`, `LC_CTYPE` and the rest); the `LC_` prefix that let any such name through is gone, so `LC_TERMINAL` or a variable passed through ssh under an `LC_` name stays out. Only the names are listed; the values are not checked. The proxy stays, since the harness reaches its model API through it, but a proxy variable whose value holds a login does not reach an agent. `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY` and `ALL_PROXY` (`PROXY_VARIABLES`, any letter case, all case variants checked) holding `@` anywhere in their value refuse the agent environment: `agent_environment` fails with `AgentEnvError::ProxyLogin`, which names the first such variable by name and never its value, "HTTPS_PROXY holds a proxy login in the runner's environment, and no proxy login reaches an agent: use a proxy that needs none in its URL, such as a local forwarding proxy that holds the login". It fails for a declared and allowed proxy variable too. `NO_PROXY`, a host list, is not checked. The rule is `@` anywhere, not a URL parse: a login ends with a literal `@` however it is percent-encoded, with a scheme or without one, and the rule does not depend on how each tool reads a value without a scheme. It errs on the side of refusal: a value holding `@` elsewhere is refused too. The runner's `has_userinfo`, which reads git settings, looks at http(s) URLs only and would miss `socks5://` and scheme-less values, so it is not reused. Checked on 2026-09-29 with curl 8.7.1 against a local listener: `http_proxy=http://fakeuser:fakepass@127.0.0.1:47931 curl http://example.invalid/` sent `Proxy-Authorization: Basic ZmFrZXVzZXI6ZmFrZXBhc3M=`, and the scheme-less `HTTPS_PROXY=fakeuser:fakepass@127.0.0.1:47932` sent the same header on its `CONNECT`. Rejected: dropping the variable, which hides the refusal (the agent goes direct, or fails on a network that forces the proxy, with no reason given); removing the login from the value, which makes the proxy answer 407 later in the run, also with no reason given. Cost: an operator whose proxy needs a login in its URL runs no agent until the proxy needs none there; before OWL-76 such runs worked, with the login reaching the agent. Other proxy names (`FTP_PROXY`, `npm_config_proxy`) are not inherited at all. Follow-up: a login in git's `http.proxy`, which the agent can read in `~/.gitconfig`, is not yet part of `AgentEnv::check`.
- **Claude Code's own variables (OWL-120, decided 2026-10-02).** Checked on 2026-10-02 with Claude Code 2.1.284 on macOS: `claude -p` with the adapter's guardrail flags and `--tools ""`, in a clean environment with a scratch `HOME` and `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, made-up values only, every request sent to a dead proxy or a local listener. The `init` event's `apiKeySource` was `none` with no login, with `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` (standing in for the agent login), and with that token plus `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` (the `model` then reads `us.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0`), `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_VERTEX` or `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_FOUNDRY`, or with Bedrock switched on by a `--settings` `env` entry, the form managed settings take. It was `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` for that variable, set in the environment or in a settings `env` entry, and `apiKeyHelper` for a settings `apiKeyHelper`. `init` has no provider field. With the token and `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` naming a local listener, the CLI sent `POST /v1/messages?beta=true` there with the token in its `Authorization` header (checked by presence). So a gateway variable hands the subscription token to whatever host it names, and the stream cannot tell gateway or provider routing from the subscription.
  - Decision, first layer: `check_names` refuses, in any letter case, every name starting with `ANTHROPIC_` or `CLAUDE_`, and `CLAUDECODE` (`HARNESS_VARIABLE_PREFIXES`, `AgentEnvError::Harness`): "ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL cannot be passed to an agent: Claude Code reads it to choose which provider, gateway or login a run uses, and agent runs log in only with the subscription token `owlshift init` keeps". The floor's credential names among them keep the floor's refusal, and `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK`, Bedrock's API key, joins the floor's list. A prefix rather than the four names the ticket listed: Claude Code reads its routing from these prefixes (the base URLs, the `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_*` switches, the skip-auth flags) and adds names between releases. Agents already inherit none of them, so a declaration was the one way in; the runner sets the few a run needs on the harness command or with `AgentEnv::set`, never as declared names. Cost: a project whose gate needs one, such as `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` pointing an SDK's tests at a mock, can no longer declare it, since the one agent environment reaches the harness too; and a personal file whose `allow_gate_env` lists one, valid before, is now invalid at load, with the variable named and this reason.
  - Decision, second layer: the adapter reads any `apiKeySource` but `none`, a value that is not a string included, as `Billing::ApiKey(source)`, kept once seen, and the executor turns it into `CredentialFinding::ApiKey`, which fails the run and points at managed settings. This is detection, not prevention: the finding is read when the run ends, so the run's work is refused but what it spent is spent. It is the one check that sees a key from outside the environment. Rejected: this check alone, blind to every routing above; reading Bedrock from the `model` string, undocumented and blind to a gateway; failing a run that reports no `apiKeySource`, which would fail every run of a release that drops the field.
  - What stays open: managed settings, set by an administrator of the machine, can still route a run through their `env` entry, undetected since it reports `none`; a run whose `init` lacks `apiKeySource` is not checked, so a release that drops the field silences the check until the fixtures are re-recorded (the contract test pins `repo_settings_ignored.jsonl` to `ApiKey("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY")`); the operator's own proxy and certificate settings, which agents inherit, are trusted.
- **Codex's own variables and interpreters' start-up code (OWL-125, decided 2026-10-02).** Checked on 2026-10-02 with codex-cli 0.154.0 installed with npm on macOS: `codex exec --skip-git-repo-check --ephemeral -s read-only "<prompt>"` under `env -i`, with a scratch `HOME` and `CODEX_HOME` per run holding a made-up `auth.json` (an API-key login, or a ChatGPT login whose tokens are an unsigned made-up JWT), every request sent to a dead proxy (`HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:9`) or to a local listener that recorded method, path, header names and JSON body keys only; each run stopped after 25 s.
  - `CODEX_REFRESH_TOKEN_URL_OVERRIDE` naming the listener, with an expired ChatGPT access token: Codex sent `POST /oauth/token` there, body keys `client_id`, `grant_type` and `refresh_token`. The login's refresh token goes to whatever host the variable names.
  - `CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL=ws://<listener>/exec`: Codex opened a websocket there, three `GET /exec` upgrade requests: a remote server to run the run's commands.
  - `CODEX_API_KEY`, with no login and a provider given on the command line (`-c model_provider=probe` with `base_url` naming the listener and `requires_openai_auth=true`, as only the runner could pass): every `POST /v1/responses` carried an `authorization` header; with no variable, with `OPENAI_API_KEY` or with `CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN` there was none, and `OPENAI_ORGANIZATION` and `OPENAI_PROJECT` added no header.
  - `OPENAI_BASE_URL`, documented as the base URL of Codex's OpenAI provider, was ignored: with an API-key login and with a ChatGPT login the run still went to `wss://api.openai.com/v1/responses` or `wss://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses`. `CODEX_AUTHAPI_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_URL`, `CODEX_APP_SERVER_CHATGPT_BASE_URL` and `CODEX_OSS_BASE_URL` (without `--oss`) were not seen to send anything within 25 s. These results hold for 0.154.0, `exec` and the two logins tried.
  - The binary's strings name more of these, not tried: `CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN` ("Agent Identity authentication"), `CODEX_REVOKE_TOKEN_URL_OVERRIDE`, `CODEX_CLOUD_TASKS_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_AGENT_IDENTITY_JWKS_BASE_URL`, `CODEX_CONNECTORS_TOKEN`, `CODEX_GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN`, `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE`, `CODEX_SQLITE_HOME`, `OPENAI_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE`, `OPENAI_FEDERATION_RULE_ID`, `OPENAI_WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_CONTEXT`.
  - `NODE_OPTIONS="--require <probe.js>"`: `codex --version` ran the probe, since the npm install starts `bin/codex.js` under Node, which spawns the native binary with its own `process.env`; `claude --version` (2.1.284, a native binary) did not. `BASH_ENV` naming a file: `/bin/bash -c true` ran it, `/bin/sh -c true` (bash 3.2 as `sh`) ran neither it nor `ENV`'s, and neither did git running `GIT_SSH_COMMAND` (macOS only; dash, Linux's usual `sh`, reads `ENV` in interactive shells only).
  - Decision, Codex: `check_names` refuses, in any letter case, every name starting with `CODEX_` or `OPENAI_` (`HARNESS_VARIABLE_PREFIXES`, now pairing each prefix with its harness; `AgentEnvError::Harness { harness, name }`): "CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL cannot be passed to an agent: it is one of Codex's own variables (`CODEX_*`, `OPENAI_*`), which choose the server, login or token endpoint a Codex run uses, and agent runs keep those of the user's Codex configuration". The Claude Code message is unchanged. `CODEX_API_KEY` joins the floor's list and keeps the floor's refusal, as `OPENAI_API_KEY` does. A prefix for the reason OWL-120 gave: the names move between releases (`OPENAI_BASE_URL`, documented as routing, did not in 0.154.0) and the binary holds dozens; `OPENAI_` is refused as a guard on that history, not because a name under it was seen to route today. `CODEX_HOME` stays inherited, so a Codex run still finds the user's login, and declaring it is refused like any `CODEX_` name, which changes nothing since its value would be the same. Cost: a gate whose tests point an OpenAI SDK at a mock (`OPENAI_BASE_URL`, `OPENAI_API_VERSION`) can no longer declare those names, and an operator who sets `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` relies on `SSL_CERT_FILE`, inherited and read by Codex too.
  - Decision, start-up code: `BASH_ENV` and `NODE_OPTIONS` are refused (`STARTUP_CODE_VARIABLES`, `AgentEnvError::StartupCode`): "NODE_OPTIONS cannot be passed to an agent: it makes an interpreter run code as it starts, in the harness or in a wrapper of git the runner starts outside the sandbox; set it in the gate command itself if the gate needs it". Two processes outside the sandbox get the agent environment: the sandbox program, and git, which the gate's checks (`Git::as_agent`) and `AgentEnv`'s lookup of the common git folder run as found on the agent's `PATH`, where it can be a bash wrapper (Nix's `makeWrapper`, an asdf or pyenv shim), and bash runs `BASH_ENV`'s file before git. Inside the sandbox, Codex's Node launcher would load `NODE_OPTIONS`'s code and could set any `CODEX_` name before it spawns the binary, which would undo the rule above. The dynamic-loader rule widens with the same reasoning: every `LD_` name, which glibc reads as its loader's, and `GCONV_PATH`, from which glibc loads code (`LOADER_PREFIXES`, `LOADER_VARIABLES`). Not refused: `PYTHONPATH`, `PYTHONSTARTUP`, `PERL5OPT`, `RUBYOPT` and `ENV`, which reach only interpreters that start inside the sandbox (the gate's and the agent's commands), where the code they load runs confined like the agent's own; a gate commonly needs `PYTHONPATH`. A gate that needs `NODE_OPTIONS` sets it in its command, `NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=4096 npm test`, with a value the repository shows. This holds while no harness is written for another interpreter and the runner starts no other program outside the sandbox with the agent environment; either change reopens it. Rejected: refusing every interpreter's start-up variable, which costs `PYTHONPATH` for no process outside the sandbox; refusing none, which leaves the Codex rule one variable from undone.
  - What stays open: no Codex harness runs agents yet, so these rules apply from its adapter on. The user's own Codex configuration can name any variable as a provider's key (`env_key`) or header (`env_http_headers`), so a variable declared under such a name reaches it; whether Codex reads a repository's own `.codex/config.toml`, the equivalent of OWL-53 for Claude Code, was not checked. The binary also reads names outside these prefixes, not tried: OpenTelemetry's exporter settings (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` and `_HEADERS`) and `SSLKEYLOGFILE`. A Codex login set only through `CODEX_API_KEY` in the user's shell does not reach an agent run: the login must live in Codex's own configuration. OWL-130, below, checks these paths and Linux's shells.
- **Codex's remaining routing paths (OWL-130, decided 2026-10-02).** Checked on 2026-10-02 with codex-cli 0.154.0 installed with npm on macOS 26.6.2: `codex exec --ephemeral --json -s read-only "<prompt>"` in a fresh git repository, with an environment built from nothing but `PATH`, `TERM`, a scratch `HOME` and `CODEX_HOME` per run holding a made-up `auth.json` (an API-key login, or a ChatGPT login whose tokens are unsigned made-up JWTs) and the variables under test, under `sandbox-exec` with loopback network only. `HTTPS_PROXY` named a local proxy that recorded each `CONNECT` and refused it, or one that answered it with TLS under a throwaway certificate authority; local listeners recorded method, path, header names, JSON body keys and which planted made-up value appeared in a header or body, never a value. Each run stopped after 20 or 25 s. The user's own `~/.codex` was not read; the probe scripts were not kept.
  - A repository's `.codex/config.toml`: read only when the user's `config.toml` trusts the project (`[projects."<path>"] trust_level = "trusted"`). Untrusted, a file naming MCP servers, a provider, an exporter and `notify` changed nothing. Trusted, Codex ignored `model_provider`, `model_providers`, `openai_base_url`, `chatgpt_base_url`, `notify` and `otel` with the warning "Ignored unsupported project-local config keys … If you want these settings to apply, manually set them in your user-level config.toml", so a repository cannot name the provider or its base URL, but it loaded `mcp_servers`: an HTTP server received the MCP `initialize` request with an `Authorization` header holding the value of the variable its `bearer_token_env_var` names and a header holding the one `env_http_headers` names, and a stdio server's command ran as Codex started. A linked worktree of a trusted checkout and a subfolder of one were trusted; a trusted folder above the repository did not make it trusted. Agents run in worktrees of the runner's own clone (`ProjectDirs::checkout`), trusted only if the user marked that clone trusted, by running Codex there and accepting its prompt for instance.
  - An exec server: the binary's configuration fields hold none; `exec_server_url` in `config.toml` stopped the run under `--strict-config` ("unknown configuration field") and was ignored without it. `experimental_thread_store_endpoint` in a trusted repository's file made Codex refuse to start ("no longer supported"). A repository's `.env` was not read, trusted or not. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` was: its `HTTPS_PROXY` replaced the environment's, sending every request to the proxy it named, while its `CODEX_EXEC_SERVER_URL` opened no websocket.
  - The user's `config.toml`: a provider's `env_key` and `env_http_headers` sent the value of any variable they name, as `Authorization: Bearer` and as the named header, in every `POST /v1/responses`; a variable missing from the environment failed the run ("Missing environment variable"). The user's `mcp_servers` loaded in a run as a trusted repository's did, with the same headers, and a stdio server started.
  - `--ignore-user-config` ("Do not load `$CODEX_HOME/config.toml`; auth still uses `CODEX_HOME`"): the trusted repository's servers were not loaded and its command did not run, the user's servers, provider and exporter did not apply, and the login still did: with the throwaway authority trusted, the ChatGPT access token reached `chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses` and the API key `api.openai.com/v1/responses`. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` was still read. A command-line override works only as an inline table: `-c 'projects={"<path>"={trust_level="untrusted"}}'`, naming the checkout or the worktree, left the servers unloaded, while the dotted form `-c 'projects."<path>".trust_level="untrusted"'`, with a path holding a dot, was silently not applied, and `-c 'projects={}'` cleared nothing.
  - OpenTelemetry: with no `[otel]` in the user's configuration, `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, `_HEADERS` and the per-signal `_ENDPOINT` names sent nothing within 25 s. With `otlp-http` exporters for logs, traces and metrics in it, each with an endpoint and a header, Codex exported logs and traces there (no metrics within 25 s); the endpoint variables did not move them, but `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` added its header to every export. Neither the login nor a header value appeared in an exported body. Under `--ignore-user-config` nothing was exported.
  - `SSLKEYLOGFILE`: through the TLS proxy, with the throwaway authority trusted through `CODEX_CA_CERTIFICATE` or `SSL_CERT_FILE` (both honoured), the handshakes completed and the requests carried the login (websocket and HTTPS `responses`, models, plugins, analytics), and no key was written: with the API-key login the file was created and stayed empty, so some client of Codex opens it, and with the ChatGPT login it was not created.
  - Found on the way: under `--ignore-user-config` too, a ChatGPT login opens the ChatGPT apps server, `POST https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/ps/mcp` with the access token, and fetches the user's plugins (`/backend-api/ps/plugins/installed`). With `--disable apps` added, the apps server was not contacted; the plugins still were. Whether that server offers a model the connectors of the user's ChatGPT account was not tried.
  - Linux, `BASH_ENV` and `ENV`: no Linux host was at hand, so the check is a test that runs on the CI's ubuntu-latest and macos-latest legs on every pull request, rather than a line saying Linux was not checked: `git_started_for_the_agent_runs_no_shell_start_up_file` (`crates/owlshift-runner/src/executor/git.rs`). The runner's git, run with the agent environment as the gate's checks run it (`Git::as_agent`), through a `#!/bin/sh` wrapper and through a bash wrapper, with `ENV` declared and allowed and naming a file that leaves a mark, and `BASH_ENV` in the runner's environment, runs an alias through `sh -c` and leaves no mark. Controls in the same test: the bash wrapper run with `BASH_ENV` and `/bin/sh -i` with `ENV` leave it, so the mark works and bash reads `BASH_ENV` in a script, which is why it stays refused. It passed on 2026-10-02 on the CI's `ubuntu-24.04` image (version 20260927.320.1) and `macos-26-arm64` image (run 37024980246, pull request #112), and locally on macOS 26.6.2. The test does not check which shell `/bin/sh` is on the image (dash by Ubuntu's default, not checked here); it covers the shells git starts, not Codex on Linux.
  - Decision, configuration: the Codex adapter passes `--ignore-user-config` on every launch, as the Claude Code adapter passes `--setting-sources ""` (OWL-53). No project is then trusted, so a repository's `.codex/config.toml` and its MCP servers stay out whatever the user trusts, and the user's own MCP servers, providers with their variable mappings, and exporters stay out too; the login still comes from `CODEX_HOME`. It belongs in the adapter's guardrail arguments and is pinned by a unit test on its command line, as `GUARDRAIL_ARGS` is for Claude Code: an acceptance item of the Codex harness (P5), with a contract test that replays a trusted repository's MCP server. The OWL-125 message's last clause, "and agent runs keep those of the user's Codex configuration", now reads "and agent runs use Codex's own servers with the login of the user's Codex (`CODEX_HOME`)". Why the flag rather than the override: the override has to name the key trust resolves under, and its dotted form fails silently; the flag does not depend on how trust is keyed, and it also keeps the user's MCP servers, which can hold tracker and forge connectors, out of agent runs. Rejected: doing nothing, since a trusted checkout lets the ticket's branch, which agents write, start a program and send declared variables to a host it names. Cost: a user whose Codex goes through a provider or gateway of their own, or whose default model and settings live in `config.toml`, gets none of it in agent runs; agent runs use Codex's own servers, as a Claude Code agent run uses only the subscription token (OWL-120). The adapter sets what a run needs on its command line; with the user's configuration gone, the sandbox, approval and shell-environment settings are Codex's defaults unless it does.
  - Decision, variables: nothing new is refused, and this rests on the decision above. With the user's configuration ignored, no configuration maps a variable into a request and no exporter exists for `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` to add to; the endpoint variables did not route a run; `SSLKEYLOGFILE` wrote no key. A gate may still declare `OTEL_*` and `SSLKEYLOGFILE`. Either reopens if the adapter loads the user's configuration, or if a release writes keys to the file.
  - What stays open: paths other than Linux's shells were checked on macOS only, with Codex 0.154.0, `exec` and `-s read-only`; the adapter's own flags (sandbox mode, `exec resume`) were not tried with `--ignore-user-config`. `$CODEX_HOME/.env` is read whatever the flag and sets variables over the agent environment, so the Codex harness must keep `CODEX_HOME` out of an agent's write reach, or the run before could route the next. Not tried: hooks, rules, skills and plugins in `CODEX_HOME` or a trusted repository, and managed configuration (`requirements.toml`). The ChatGPT apps server above is the Codex harness's to settle. OWL-131, below, tried them and `exec resume`.
  - **The paths left untried (OWL-131, checked 2026-10-15).** codex-cli 0.154.0 on macOS 26.6, `codex exec` from a linked worktree of a scratch repository, under `env -i` with a scratch `HOME` and `CODEX_HOME`, no login, and `HTTPS_PROXY` on a dead port. The model was a local stand-in for the Responses API, named with `-c model_provider` and `-c model_providers.<name>`; it logged each request's body and played one scripted tool call. The scratch `config.toml` trusted the checkout and the worktree. Each vector was planted in the repository (committed) and in `CODEX_HOME`, and each was first seen to fire in a control run: the adapter's flags without `--ignore-user-config`. The probe scripts were not kept.
    - MCP. In the control, the repository's and `CODEX_HOME`'s stdio servers started, their tools were offered, and the scripted call reached the repository's. Under the adapter's flags, neither started nor was offered, and Codex refused the call (`unsupported call`) without an item in the stream. Recorded as `trusted_repo_mcp` and `trusted_repo_mcp_control`; `tests/codex_fixtures.rs` checks the pair, and that the recorded command line is `command()`'s, so a change of flags fails until the pair is recorded again.
    - Hooks: `hooks.json` and `[hooks]` in `config.toml`, in the repository's `.codex/` and in `CODEX_HOME`, on `SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse` and `Stop`, each leaving a mark. A hook runs only once trusted, a trust Codex keeps in the user's `config.toml`: in the control none ran until `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` was added, and then all did. Under the adapter's flags none ran; with that flag added, those of `$CODEX_HOME/hooks.json` ran and the repository's did not. The adapter never passes it, and a run's `CODEX_HOME` is its own. Both files in one folder are reported as an error item.
    - Plugins: a plugin of the personal marketplace (`~/.agents/plugins/marketplace.json`) installed into `CODEX_HOME` with `codex plugin add`, carrying a skill, an MCP server and hooks. The control, with the hook-trust flag, loaded all three; the adapter's flags, with it or without, none, the installed plugins being listed in `config.toml`.
    - Rules: a trusted repository's `.codex/rules/` file forbidding `touch` rejected the command in a control without `--ignore-rules`. Under `--ignore-user-config` alone the command ran, the repository being untrusted, and so it did under the adapter's flags.
    - `exec resume`: `codex exec --json --ignore-user-config … resume --last -`, the flags before the subcommand (`resume --help` does not list the flag, but takes it), resumed the same thread with no MCP server, also for a session started without the flag; resuming without it started both. The adapter never resumes: `--ephemeral` keeps no session.
    - Skills: under the adapter's flags of then, the model's instructions listed the skills (name, description and path) of the worktree's `.agents/skills` and `.codex/skills`, of `$CODEX_HOME/skills` and of `~/.agents/skills`, trusted or not. A run's `CODEX_HOME` is its own and the sandbox closes `~/.agents` on macOS (C2), but the worktree is the ticket's branch, which agents write: one run could plant a skill for the next critic. `-c skills.include_instructions=false` removed all four listings. A skill's MCP dependencies (`agents/openai.yaml`) were not installed by `exec`, even with the skill named in the prompt.
    - `AGENTS.md`: `-c project_doc_max_bytes=0` removed the worktree's from the instructions and kept `$CODEX_HOME`'s, which a run's own home does not hold. This settles C2's open item.
    - Managed configuration: the binary names `/etc/codex/config.toml`, `/etc/codex/managed_config.toml` and `/etc/codex/requirements.toml`, and on macOS the `com.openai.codex` preferences (`config_toml_base64`, `requirements_toml_base64`). The `/etc/codex` files need an administrator: the maintainer ran, with `sudo` on 2026-10-15, probes that planted them in the same lab and removed them after.
      - `config.toml` and `managed_config.toml`, each declaring a stdio MCP server and a `SessionStart` hook that leave a mark: under the adapter's flags both servers started and were offered to the model, and both hooks ran, with no hook trust and no `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust`; the same as in the control, which also had the repository's and `CODEX_HOME`'s servers.
      - `requirements.toml` with `allowed_sandbox_modes = ["read-only"]` and a managed hooks folder (`[hooks] managed_dir`): every run, the adapter's and the control, stopped before its thread with exit 1, `approval_policy = "never"` cannot be used because requirements do not allow `sandbox_mode = "danger-full-access"`. Such a file fails every Codex run closed; the managed hooks folder stays untried behind that refusal.
      - Overrides, tried on the layers a run can reach without an administrator: `-c 'mcp_servers={}'` removed nothing, the layers merging key by key; `-c mcp_servers.<name>.enabled=false` turned off a named server; `--disable hooks` stopped every hook, trusted or not. Whether either beats the `/etc/codex` files was not tried.
      - MDM, the `com.openai.codex` preferences, was not tried: it would change the preferences of the maintainer's Codex desktop app.
      - Decision, with the maintainer on 2026-10-15: `/etc/codex` and MDM are the operator's, as Claude Code's managed settings are (runtime and operations, "Codex routing"). Only an administrator writes them and an agent cannot plant them; the MCP servers and hooks they declare reach agent runs, and a `requirements.toml` refusing `danger-full-access` stops every Codex run. `owlshift doctor` is to report both (a follow-up).
        - Done by OWL-314 (2026-10-16): when Codex is a required harness on the `PATH`, doctor's `codex machine config` line names the enabled `mcp_servers` and the `[hooks]` events of `/etc/codex/config.toml` and `managed_config.toml` in a warning, and its `codex requirements` line, shown when `requirements.toml` exists, reports one whose `allowed_sandbox_modes` leaves out `danger-full-access` as a Codex that is not ready, a warning, with the administrator's fix. Since OWL-347 these lines show only when the personal file declares `[harnesses.codex]`, and no run reads the file: `codex_critic` is kept, unwired, for OWL-348. A `[hooks] managed_dir` is named in a warning. A file doctor cannot read, or holding a value of a shape Codex's files do not have, is a warning naming it; a TOML error shows its line only, since the file can hold header values. The parsing is `owlshift_runner::codex_machine`, for the run-time check of a usable Codex to share. Not read: the `com.openai.codex` managed preferences, the requirements' other keys (`allowed_approval_policies`, `allowed_login_methods`, its `mcp_servers` list), and a `hooks.json` in `/etc/codex`, none of them tried. At run time the same parsing decides whether the critic runs on Codex (OWL-315, 2026-10-16; the check is OWL-303's `codex_machine::codex_critic`, see "F · Design and Design review in the runner" below): a `requirements.toml` that leaves out `danger-full-access` sends the critic to Claude Code, and so does one that cannot be read, is not valid TOML or holds a shape Owlshift does not know. That differs from doctor, which only warns on such a file: a run that may stop before it starts is not tried, while doctor reports without blocking. A missing file, as on Windows, leaves Codex usable. Covered by `codex_runs_the_critic_only_when_installed_logged_in_and_allowed`.
    - Decision, with the maintainer on 2026-10-15: the adapter also passes `-c skills.include_instructions=false` and `-c project_doc_max_bytes=0` on every launch, as the Claude Code adapter loads no `CLAUDE.md`, rule or skill of the worktree (OWL-53). The brief carries the project's rules, read at the base commit (`stack.rules`, OWL-61), so nothing the ticket's branch writes reaches the critic as an instruction; the critic still reads the branch's files, as what it reviews. Cost: Codex's bundled skills are not listed either. Pinned by `a_critic_run_is_launched_with_the_flags_of_the_design` and by the pair's command-line check.
    - What stays open: these results hold for 0.154.0 on macOS; an upgrade needs them again, since `tested.rs` warns and does not refuse. The Linux job replays the recordings only. The two new flags were not run on the subscription with a real model: `tests/codex_live.rs`, run by the maintainer, is that check. A repository's `.codex/agents/` definitions were not tried. OWL-132 keeps the apps server, a ChatGPT login's remote plugins and `$CODEX_HOME/.env`.
  - **The apps server, plugins and `$CODEX_HOME/.env` (OWL-132, checked 2026-10-16).** codex-cli 0.154.0 on macOS 26.6, the maintainer's ChatGPT login, the adapter's flags, a scratch repository, and a `CODEX_HOME` of the run's own holding a copy of `auth.json`, written back under `codex_login_kept`; `auth.json` was unchanged after every run. The model was asked to list the tools it could call and the ChatGPT connectors it could reach.
    - Three runs by a throwaway script under `env -i` (`HOME`, `PATH`, `USER`, `LANG`, `CODEX_HOME`), without `--output-schema`. Under the adapter's flags of then, the model reached no connector ("NONE", on an account whose connectors were not inventoried). The apps feature added four tools: `list_mcp_resources`, `list_mcp_resource_templates`, `read_mcp_resource` and `request_plugin_install`, the model's way into the server that holds the access token. With `--disable apps` the four were gone and nothing else changed. With `--disable plugins --disable remote_plugin` added too, the list was the same as with `--disable apps` alone.
    - Through `Executor::run`, with the adapter's full command line, the run without `--disable apps` started its turn and never ended it: twice, until the executor's ten-minute timeout, confined by the read-only profile and unconfined alike. The run with the flag ended in seconds. By hand, the same command line without the flag also ended in seconds. The cause was not isolated.
    - Recorded as `apps_disabled` and `apps_disabled_control` by `tests/codex_live.rs` (`a_live_critic_run_is_offered_no_tool_of_the_apps_server`), which spawns `codex exec` itself for that reason. The control's command line differs by `--disable apps` alone. `tests/codex_fixtures.rs` checks that the adapter's list holds none of the four tools, the control's holds all four, and both hold the shell's `exec_command`; the adapter's recorded command line is `command()`'s.
    - Decision: the adapter passes `--disable apps` on every launch. The plugin flags are not passed: nothing they change was observed. Other tools the critic is offered under the adapter's flags are left for a ticket of their own: web search (`web__run`), image generation, the collaboration tools (`spawn_agent` and the rest) and `request_user_input`. OWL-316, below, decided them.
    - `$CODEX_HOME/.env`: a run's `CODEX_HOME` is its own (OWL-299), so the user's `.env` is never in it. `tests/confinement.rs` (`a_codex_run_gets_its_own_home_and_its_result_from_its_last_message`) shows two things on Seatbelt and on the Linux job's `bwrap`. First, a planted `.env` in the user's `CODEX_HOME` can be neither read nor written by the run. Second, the run's home holds only `auth.json` when the run starts, and the run can write a `.env` there, which goes away with the run.
    - What stays open: a ChatGPT login still fetches the user's installed plugins (OWL-130), and the model is offered no plugin tool. Whether the model can reach ChatGPT connectors with the apps server on was not tried on an account that has some, and it no longer matters with the server off. Model self-report is the evidence: the control's positive list is what keeps it from proving nothing.
  - **The built-in tools a critic run keeps (OWL-316, checked 2026-10-16).** codex-cli 0.154.0 on macOS 26.6, the maintainer's ChatGPT login, effort `low`. The candidate flags came from the binary: `codex features list` names `image_generation`, `multi_agent` and `multi_agent_v2`, and the config's `web_search` takes `disabled`, `indexed` or `live`. Two rounds of a throwaway probe script, run by the maintainer under `env -i` (`HOME`, `PATH`, `USER`, `LANG`, `CODEX_HOME` the user's own), the adapter's flags of then plus the candidates, `--skip-git-repo-check` in an empty folder, the model asked to list its tools as in OWL-132.

A second run asked the model to call `request_user_input`: the call failed at once with "request_user_input is unavailable in Default mode", also logged on standard error, and the run ended in 12 s with exit status 0.
    - Round 2, the collaboration tools only: `--disable multi_agent_v2` alone, then with `--disable multi_agent` too. Both lists were the same as round 1's, collaboration tools included, and no tool of the binary's first multi-agent version (`send_input`, `resume_agent`, `close_agent`) appeared. The tools offered are those of the binary's `multi_agents_v2` handlers, and the server's model catalog carries a `multi_agent_version` per model, which may be what switches them on whatever the features say; not isolated.
    - Recorded as `critic_tools` and `critic_tools_control` by `tests/codex_live.rs` (`a_live_critic_run_is_offered_only_the_tools_it_keeps`), outside the executor as OWL-132's pair; the control's command line differs by the two flags alone. Run by the maintainer on 2026-10-16, it confirms the shipped flags without any multi-agent one: the two lists differ by `web__run` and `image_gen__imagegen` alone, and both keep the collaboration tools. `tests/codex_fixtures.rs` checks that the adapter's list holds neither `web__run` nor `image_gen__imagegen`, the control's holds both, and both hold `exec_command`; the adapter's recorded command line is `command()`'s.
    - Decision, tool by tool. Turned off: web search, `-c web_search="disabled"`, since it searches on the operator's account and read-only roles run without network where the harness supports it (architecture, section 8); image generation, `--disable image_generation`, since a critic returns JSON and an image spends quota. Kept: the shell (`exec_command`, `write_stdin`), which the critic reads the worktree with, under the read-only profile; `apply_patch`, for which no flag was found and whose writes the profile refuses (C2, `write_refused`); `view_image`, the clock and `sleep`, local, a sleep bounded by the executor's timeout; `functions.exec` and `functions.wait`, not traced to a feature, which run under the same profile and timeout; `request_user_input`, which exec mode refuses at once; `request_user_input_async`, not called, for which no flag was found either and whose run the executor's timeout bounds; and the collaboration tools, which no flag removes and which the runner refuses when called (OWL-317). Neither multi-agent flag is passed: neither changed anything observed.
    - What stays open: a critic run can still start sub-agents, which multiply its cost under the same profile and timeout; OWL-317, below, refuses a critic run that calls them, as far as Codex's stream shows it. The results hold for 0.154.0 and this account's model catalog; an upgrade or a catalog change needs them again. Model self-report is the evidence, as in OWL-132.
  - **A critic run's collaboration tools (OWL-317, checked 2026-10-16).** codex-cli 0.154.0 on macOS 26.6, the maintainer's ChatGPT login, effort `low`, the adapter's command line, `codex exec` spawned by `tests/codex_live.rs` outside the executor as OWL-132's runs, with a `CODEX_HOME` of the run's own holding a copy of `auth.json`, unchanged after every run. Until then no recording held a collaboration item; the binary's strings name an exec item `collab_tool_call` with `tool` (`spawn_agent`, `send_input`, `wait`, `close_agent`), `sender_thread_id`, `receiver_thread_ids`, `prompt`, `agents_states` and `status`.
    - Run 1, the model asked to start one sub-agent replying PONG, call `list_agents`, then wait for it with `wait_agent`: done in 16 s with exit status 0 and the result's summary "PONG". The stream held two agent messages and one `collab_tool_call` item, started then completed, with `tool` `wait`, `receiver_thread_ids` and `agents_states` empty and `prompt` null. `spawn_agent` and `list_agents` left no event; the item ids ran on with no gap. The usage, 36,640 input tokens of which 26,752 cached, is four requests of the main thread, so the sub-agent's tokens are most likely not counted in `turn.completed`; not isolated.
    - Run 2, the same with `-c agents.max_depth=0`: the binary refuses `agents.max_concurrent_threads_per_session` below 1 but checks no bound on `max_depth`, and holds "Agent depth limit reached. Solve the task yourself." Stream and usage as run 1's. Inconclusive: a refused spawn tells the model to do the task itself, and the task was to say PONG.
    - Runs 3 and 4, the model asked to give the exact outputs of its `spawn_agent` and `wait_agent` calls, with and without the depth flag: both streams stopped after the `wait` item completed, with no event for five minutes, until the test's watchdog stopped them; neither ended its turn. The cause was not isolated.
    - Codex's default multi-agent instructions, in the binary, tell the model not to spawn sub-agents unless the user or applicable `AGENTS.md` or skill instructions ask: the critic's prompt never asks, and `AGENTS.md` and skills are dropped (OWL-131).
    - Recorded as `critic_collaboration` by `tests/codex_live.rs` (`a_live_critic_run_that_starts_a_sub_agent_is_seen`), a fifth run with run 1's prompt, which ended as run 1 did: 14 s, the `wait` item alone, 36,661 input tokens. `record()` now replaces every UUID, the sub-agents' thread ids included. `tests/codex_fixtures.rs` checks that its replay reports the `wait` call, that no event of it is a `spawn_agent` item, that no other recording reports one, and that its command line is `command()`'s.
    - Decision, with the maintainer on 2026-10-16: the runner refuses a critic run whose stream carries a `collab_tool_call` item, whatever its tool (architecture section 5, "Its sub-agents"). The adapter's transcript lists the tools; the executor's Codex harness turns a completed run into a harness failure naming them and adds them to a failed run's reason; a usage limit stays one. A partial guard, accepted: a spawn without a wait is not seen.
    - What stays open: a sub-agent started and never waited for; a critic that hangs after its wait, and stopping a run at its first `collab_tool_call` item, both settled by OWL-319, which stops the run's whole process tree at that item (architecture section 5, "Its sub-agents"). `agents.max_depth=0` was settled by OWL-320, below: it refuses nothing. The results hold for 0.154.0 and this account's model catalog.
  - **A depth limit of zero for a critic run (OWL-320, checked 2026-10-16).** codex-cli 0.154.0 on macOS 26.6, the maintainer's ChatGPT login, effort `low`. Two pairs run by `tests/codex_live.rs` (`a_depth_limit_of_zero_does_not_stop_a_live_critic_run_from_starting_a_sub_agent`) from an agent's worktree, each a probe on the adapter's command line plus `-c agents.max_depth=0` and a control without it, spawned outside the executor as OWL-317's runs, with a `CODEX_HOME` of the run's own holding a copy of `auth.json` and a five-minute watchdog.
    - The sub-agent's task is one the parent cannot do: Codex gives each shell command its thread's id as `CODEX_THREAD_ID` (the binary's exec environment), so only another thread writes an id other than the parent's. A sub-agent at work is a UUIDv7 thread id that is not the parent's and whose embedded time lies between the parent's and the run's end. A refusal is Codex's message, "Agent depth limit reached", on standard error or relayed by the model. An absence proves neither.
    - Pair 1, the sub-agent asked to `echo` the id and reply with it. Probe: one agent message and a `wait` item, started then completed, then no event until the watchdog stopped the run at 300 s; standard error empty. Control: done in about 15 s with `spawn_agent` itself failing, "collab spawn failed: no thread with id: …" on an id that was no sub-agent's, logged on standard error as `ERROR codex_core::tools::router: error=collab spawn failed: …` and given as the summary. It settled nothing.
    - Two findings of pair 1. A collaboration tool's error reaches standard error through Codex's tool router. Under `--ephemeral` a spawn can fail outright, most likely one that forks the parent's turns and looks up the parent's thread, which `--ephemeral` keeps nowhere; not isolated. OWL-317's recorded spawn, with standard error empty, did not fail so.
    - Pair 2, the sub-agent asked to append the id to a file in the run's folder, `spawn_agent`'s `fork_turns` set to `"none"`, and the file read whatever the run came to. Probe: the file held one id, not the parent's, made 6.6 s after it; standard error empty. Control: the same, 5.4 s after its parent. Both streams held one agent message and the `wait` item, then no event until the watchdog: neither the sub-agent's command nor its messages reach the parent's stream.
    - Result: `-c agents.max_depth=0` does not refuse `spawn_agent` in a critic run. Whether 0.154.0's multi-agent tools read the key at all was not isolated. Four runs; the one that ended used 18,287 input tokens (8,960 cached), the others reported no usage.
    - Decision: the adapter does not pass the flag, and OWL-317's guard stays the only check (architecture section 5, "Its sub-agents"). The live test stays and asserts that both runs show a sub-agent at work, so a release where the flag refuses fails it. Its runs are not recorded: a hung stream is no fixture.
    - What stays open: no flag found keeps a critic from starting sub-agents. The hang after a `wait` was seen in five of the eight runs that waited (OWL-317's runs 3 and 4, three of OWL-320's); since OWL-319 the runner stops such a run at its `wait` item.

- **For the executor (OWL-15).** In `owlshift_runner::agent_env`, `AgentEnv::from_runner` builds the environment and `AgentEnv::check` runs git and gh as the agent would, in the worktree. It reports credential variables (a token variable holding the placeholder passes), a password from `git credential fill`, a login from `gh auth token`, and the git settings that carry a credential the environment cannot remove: an http(s) URL with user info in a remote or a URL rewrite, an extra HTTP header, keys redacted. A probe that fails or times out counts as a finding. A finding stops the run before it starts; `AgentEnv::apply` then replaces the command's environment, and after the run `mcp_findings` fails a run whose `init` listed any MCP server. `floor::check_agent_environment` stays the check of declared names: on a whole agent environment it would flag the placeholder.
- **Tests.** `crates/owlshift-testkit/tests/agent_credentials.rs` plants token variables, a parent session's variables, a git credential helper, a gh login and repository settings carrying credentials on a host. The check finds each in the host's own environment and none in the agent environment, and a child spawned with it sees none of those variables. It runs the real git and gh on the three CI platforms, gh being required there. gh's first start on a fresh Windows runner took up to 14 s, past the probe's 10 s (OWL-56): every test binary whose probes run gh, this one and `on_demand.rs`, first starts it once through `owlshift_testkit::gh::warm_up`, which is why this test lives in the bench (OWL-70). The MCP half needs a harness login, which CI lacks: it rests on the adapter always passing the flags, unit tests on the `init` shapes above, and `crates/owlshift-runner/tests/agent_live.rs`, run by hand. On 2026-09-28 on the maintainer's machine (claude.ai connectors configured, gh login in the keyring, `osxkeychain` helper, SSH keys, launched from inside a Claude Code session) it passed: no credential finding, and the run completed on the subscription (`apiKeySource: none`) with no MCP server.
- **What stays open.** This closes what an agent reaches without going around Owlshift: its tool list, its environment, and git and gh as they run from its shell. A process that goes looking, by unsetting an override, reading a token file or querying the system keychain, is stopped by the operating system's sandbox of OWL-41, below. A harness configured through environment variables loses the ones it would inherit, as it already loses user settings to C1's flags. Claude Code's own (`ANTHROPIC_*`, `CLAUDE_*`, `CLAUDECODE`) cannot be put back either: since OWL-120 (above) they are refused as gate variables, declared or allowed, so no gateway (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`), Bedrock, Vertex or Foundry routing reaches a Claude Code run from the environment; the machine's managed settings still can. Any other variable reaches the harness only as a gate variable the project declares and the operator allows in `allow_gate_env` (OWL-63, OWL-75): a run has one agent environment, so a gate variable reaches the harness too. Reworded on 2026-10-02 (OWL-121): the previous text said such a harness loses its variables (a gateway `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, Bedrock or Vertex settings), which held only for inherited ones. From OWL-44 until OWL-120, a declared Claude Code variable off the credential floor, such as `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, did reach the harness: with no allowance until OWL-63, with the operator's after it. Native Windows was not run live (decision D9: WSL2 first).

**OWL-41 — confining agent runs: live checks.** Run on 2026-09-29 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2, Apple silicon, Claude Code 2.1.284). The secrets were fake and made for the check: a gh-shaped `hosts.yml` and a `.env` planted in a scratch directory, and an item in a throwaway keychain file created with `security create-keychain`, named by its path in every call, never added to the search list, and deleted afterwards. The login keychain was not read. Claude Code's own login was only checked for presence, with `claude auth status`.

- Without confinement, `cat` reads the planted file and `security find-generic-password -s <item> -w <keychain file>` prints the fake secret. gh stores its login the same way, through the `security` program, and so does Claude Code (the binary runs `security find-generic-password -a <user> -w -s "Claude Code-credentials"`): any process of the user that runs `security` reads either one without a prompt.
- `/usr/bin/sandbox-exec` is present, although its manual page calls it deprecated. A profile applied with it cannot be stacked: `sandbox-exec` inside `sandbox-exec` fails with `sandbox_apply: Operation not permitted`.
- A confined process cannot exec a setuid binary (OWL-110). Checked on 2026-10-01 on the maintainer's machine (macOS 26.6.2): `/bin/ps` is setuid root (`-rwsr-xr-x root wheel`), and `sandbox-exec -p '(version 1)(allow default)' /bin/ps -o stat= -p <pid>` fails with `execvp() of '/bin/ps' failed: Operation not permitted` (exit 71), while the same command unconfined succeeds. Seatbelt refuses the exec whatever the profile allows, so this is a macOS limit, not a rule to relax. `/usr/bin/pgrep` is not setuid and is not affected. The first real `owlshift do` (OWL-107) stopped `blocked` on it: four CLI tests read a process's state with `ps`, so the gate could not pass in the sandbox. The tests now read the state with `sysctl` (`KERN_PROC_PID`) on macOS and from `/proc/<pid>/stat` on Linux, and run no `ps`. Any command a gate runs under confinement must not be setuid on macOS.
- A runner-applied profile of `(allow default)` plus denials works as follows:
  - `(deny file-read* (subpath <dir>))` makes `cat` fail with status 1.
  - Denying the keychain in either of two ways makes `security find-generic-password` fail at once, with status 44 ("could not be found") and no prompt: `(deny file-read* file-write*)` on the keychain files, or `(deny mach-lookup)` of `com.apple.SecurityServer` and `com.apple.securityd.xpc`. The client opens a keychain file itself.
  - The last matching rule wins. With the home denied and one folder allowed again (`file-read-data` and `file-read-xattr`), the folder's files can be read, `.env` and the token file cannot, listing the home fails, and `stat` and `pwd -P` still work.
  - Corrected on 2026-09-29, checked live the same day. The last matching rule wins only among rules naming the same operations. An operation named (`file-read-data`) outranks the wildcard (`file-read*`), whatever the order. A later `(allow file-read* …)` does not reopen a folder an earlier `(deny file-read-data …)` closed: a file in it could be neither listed nor read. A later `(deny file-read* …)` does not close a file inside a folder an earlier `(allow file-read-data …)` opened: a planted credential file in an opened folder was read. The `pwd -P` above was the shell's builtin, which answers from `$PWD`. The programs `/bin/pwd -P`, Python's `os.getcwd()` and git fail with "Operation not permitted" in a folder whose parent is closed, since `getcwd` lists each folder above the working directory. Allowing `file-read-data` on each such parent alone (`literal`: its names, not its files) lets them work.
  - Such a profile gives no write restriction of its own: a write into the home succeeded.
