A watchdog thread stops the tree at the executor's deadline, a failed run; the tree is stopped again after every run, before the result is read, so nothing the run left running changes what is checked. `ClaudeHarness` uses the adapter's `command` and `drive`, with the role's prompt followed by one line naming the brief file; an MCP server in its `init` event fails the run, and so does a completed run whose `init` reports another permission mode than the one asked (OWL-346). `drive_plain` serves a harness without a driver of its own, such as the fake harness. The executor's own git commands run through `owlshift_platform::process::run_command`, also as the root of a process tree, stopped after 120 s, with their whole output: the probes' 64 KiB cap would cut a large repository's status and blind the isolation check (OWL-48). The watchdog stays for the runs whose output is streamed to a log, the harness and the gate's commands.
- **Outcome**, in this order: an isolation violation quarantines the run, whatever else happened, and the core parks the ticket; then the deadline, an MCP server loaded, a usage limit (an interruption, with its reset time) and a harness failure. Otherwise the result decides: `Finished` needs the harness to report completion and a valid `result.json`, read without following a link and at most 1 MiB, validated against its contract, with `pr.branch` equal to the run's branch and every artifact readable (OWL-26, OWL-35); a read-only run that completed without one is read from its last message, by the rule of architecture section 6 (OWL-323). A harness failure with a valid result is still a failure, and so is an exit status 0 without one.
- **Isolation**, after every run. The main checkout: its HEAD commit and branch, and every entry of `git status --porcelain=v2 --untracked-files=all --ignored=matching` with the content hash of each file it lists (tracked and staged changes, untracked files, ignored ones such as `.env`; an ignored directory counts by its presence). The repository's shared `config`, `config.worktree`, `hooks/` and `info/`, where a planted hook would later run code in the user's checkout. Every ref but the run's branch and the remote-tracking refs: tags, other branches, `refs/stash`. The other worktrees' entries of the common directory (`worktrees/<name>/...`, the run's own left out), which hold what another ticket's worktree stages (`index`) and sees (`HEAD`): `Violation::WorktreeEntries` (OWL-339). The walk keeps raw file names, so a name that is not UTF-8 is a key of its own, and violations spell paths with `/` on every system; a file is compared whole, a shared file by its bytes or, past 1 MiB, its SHA-256, a file of an entry always by its size and SHA-256; a path that vanishes mid-read, such as a lock file, is absent, not an error; one read of the shared files takes in at most 1 GiB of files in all (`isolation::READ_BUDGET`), one read of the entries at most 1 GiB from each entry and 64 GiB from all of them (`ENTRY_BUDGET`, `ENTRIES_BUDGET`), past which the check could not run, so a run that got past the sandbox cannot keep it reading with a huge or sparse file. They are compared with the refs, after the file-system checks, so a changed hook ends the check before they are read. And the worktree: still on its branch, at a commit that descends from the one the run started from, or, for a rebase, from its new base. A check that cannot run counts as a violation. Nothing is written: `--no-optional-locks`, and `hash-object` without `-w`. Before any git runs, two checks read the file system alone (OWL-59): the worktree's `.git` link (see "The worktree's `.git` link" under OWL-20 below), and the shared git files against the snapshot, from the common directory resolved before the run. A violation there ends the check with no git run, so a command a run planted in the shared configuration, such as a clean filter that `git status` of the checkout would run, never runs with the runner's environment. Every command of the runner's git, the executor's and `owlshift do`'s alike, runs with `-c core.hooksPath=<a folder that does not exist> -c core.fsmonitor=false`: no hook and no file-system monitor runs, `worktree add`'s `post-checkout` included. The rest of the runner's environment is kept, since the person's own configuration, such as `safe.directory` or what the fetch authenticates with, must still apply (decided 2026-09-29). Two ways past those checks are closed (OWL-50, decided 2026-09-29), both checked live that day with git 2.54.0. A nested repository in the main checkout, with a clean filter in its own `config`, a `* filter=<it>` attribute and a changed file, and its gitlink staged in the checkout's index (or committed), made the hardened `git status` start git in it and run the filter three times; the main checkout's status now runs with `--ignore-submodules=dirty`, which starts no git in a submodule and still lists a gitlink added or moved, where `all` hides the gitlink too and `untracked` still runs the filter. The monitor stays off in a nested git either way: `-c core.fsmonitor=false` reaches it through `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`. A `commondir` file written in the main checkout's git directory made git there read another repository's configuration, and run its filter; `commondir` is now one of the shared git files, so it stops the check before any git. The check still runs with the runner's environment, not through `Git::as_agent` as the gate's checks do: the agent environment strips credential variables, not the runner's files, which a program that did run would reach anyway; and the snapshot, taken before the run, would need the same environment for ignored files (`core.excludesFile` found through `HOME` or `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`) to compare equal. What that git can still run is what the checked configuration already named, such as a filter driver configured before the run (see the limits below); this settles the check's part of the follow-up under OWL-16.
- **Its limits.** The check assumes nobody else changes the main checkout during a run: a person editing or committing there looks like a breach. `owlshift do` (OWL-20) therefore hangs worktrees off a checkout nobody edits, a clone under Owlshift's data directory, so that a person keeps working in theirs (S12; see "`owlshift do`" below). It does not see changes outside the repository, a file inside an ignored directory, an edit inside a submodule's work tree (OWL-50; the checkout never populates one), an edit hidden by an index flag (`assume-unchanged`, `skip-worktree`), or a process that left the tree with `setsid`; confining an agent is the operating system's job (OWL-41). Its checks before any git trust the configuration they compare to be the whole of it: a file an `include.path` of the snapshot's configuration names goes unseen (a change anywhere in a shared file is seen: past the first MiB the whole file is compared by its digest), and a filter driver configured before the run still runs on the checkout's changed files, with whatever script it names; the dedicated clone (OWL-20) has none of these.
- **Git's own housekeeping** (OWL-197, decided 2026-10-08). Once a repository holds enough loose objects, `git commit` and `git fetch` start `git maintenance run --auto` in the background, where it outlives them; its `git repack` runs `git update-server-info`, which writes the shared `info/refs`, so a run during which it lands was quarantined for nothing. CI's Ubuntu leg quarantined `refusals_resolver_passes_and_a_late_comment_stop_at_the_ceiling` that way once: the bench's stand-in agent, which runs without the sandbox, had committed. A confined agent cannot write `info/`, but the runner's own `fetch` in `project::sync_checkout`, just before the first snapshot, starts the same maintenance outside the sandbox. Checked on 2026-10-08 with git 2.54.0 (Apple Git-157) on macOS, in scratch repositories: `commit` and `fetch` started it (a geometric or cruft repack, then a multi-pack index) once two loose objects lay in `objects/17`, about 800 in all, not with one there at 400, so whether it fires depends on object ids; `worktree add`, `worktree prune`, `hash-object -w`, `mktree`, `commit-tree`, `update-ref`, `push` and `status` started none. With `-c repack.updateServerInfo=false` on `commit` or `fetch`, the repack still ran and `info/refs` stayed absent; without it, `info/refs` was written. With `maintenance.geometric-repack.auto = -1` added, locally, to the bench's git configuration, that test was quarantined at the same run as on CI (attempt 8, the first whose stand-in commits), and trace2 showed the stand-in's `commit` starting the maintenance, then the repack; with the agent environment's setting below, the same bench passed. Decision: git that Owlshift starts does not update the server information, and the check stays as it is. Every command of the runner's git runs with `-c repack.updateServerInfo=false` beside its hook and monitor settings (`executor::git`), which reaches the maintenance and the repack git starts itself; the agent environment carries the same as a second command-scope entry of OWL-22's overrides (`GIT_CONFIG_COUNT=2`). A confined agent's write was refused anyway, but its git printed `error: unable to update .git/info/refs: Permission denied` (checked with `info/` read-only), which a model may chase. Git knows the setting from 2.36.0 (`builtin/repack.c` at the tags v2.35.0 and v2.36.0, read on 2026-10-08); `owlshift do` already needs a newer git, for `symbolic-ref --no-recurse`, absent at v2.36.0 (new in 2.39.0, the floor `owlshift doctor` checks: OWL-199). The acceptance holds by prevention: a run that writes `info/refs` itself, or whose git ignores the setting, is still quarantined. Not chosen: leaving `info/refs` out of the check, or checking only `info/exclude`, `info/attributes` and `info/sparse-checkout`, which lets a guarded folder change unseen; `maintenance.auto=false`, which would leave Owlshift's long-lived clone with no housekeeping, the runner's fetch and the agents' commits being the only commands that start it there; maintenance in the fetch's foreground, within its 120 s deadline. Tests: `executor::git`'s `the_runners_git_leaves_the_server_info_alone`, where a `gc` through the runner's git writes no `info/refs` and plain git's does, and `tests/isolation.rs`, where a `gc` by the agent's git during a run passes while a `gc` with `repack.updateServerInfo=true` (plain git's until OWL-240 below) and a written `info/exclude` are found. A `gc` stands in for the automatic maintenance: the same repack, run as a child process, on every git version and platform. Since OWL-339 (decided 2026-10-19), the runner's git also keeps that maintenance from expiring reflogs and worktree entries, whose rewrite of other entries' `logs/HEAD` the isolation check guards: see the OWL-339 live check (e). The agent environment carries the same four settings as four more command-scope entries (`GIT_CONFIG_COUNT=6`), as a review of the pull request asked: in the sandbox the reflog step of an agent's maintenance would try to lock the `HEAD` of other entries, which it refuses, and print errors a model may chase, the reason `repack.updateServerInfo` is there.
- **The bench's own git** (OWL-240, decided 2026-10-12). CI's Ubuntu leg (git 2.55.0) quarantined `a_trivial_conflict_is_rebased_on_the_fast_tier_and_pushed_by_the_runner` once more for `info/refs` ([run 38276043677](https://github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/actions/runs/38276043677), attempt 1, PR #207; the rerun passed). The writer was neither the runner's git nor the agent's, both covered by OWL-197, but the bench's: the test plays the rebase agent itself, inside the run's window, with `GitEnv`, whose configuration lacked the setting. Checked on 2026-10-12 with git 2.54.0 (Apple Git-157) on macOS: trace2 of a scratch rebase shows `git rebase --continue` running a child `git commit`, which starts `git maintenance run --auto --detach`, then `git repack -d -l --geometric=2 --write-midx`, which writes the checkout's `info/refs`; with `repack.updateServerInfo=false` in the bench's file the repack still ran and `info/refs` stayed absent. Reproduced on the bench by adding `maintenance.geometric-repack.auto = -1`, `maintenance.autoDetach = false` and `gc.autoDetach = false` to its configuration, locally (geometric repacking is the default maintenance of this git; an older one may never repack under these settings): without the fix, that test was quarantined at every run with CI's message, and so were `a_non_trivial_conflict_goes_to_the_build_routing_at_once` and, in `tests/isolation.rs`, `a_runs_own_work_passes_and_every_breach_is_found` and `concurrent_runs_pass_and_every_other_ref_stays_guarded`, all of which run the bench's git within a run's window; with it, every test of `owlshift-testkit` passed (`on_demand` 69, scenarios 33, isolation 6). That Ubuntu's git takes the same path is inferred from the same message on the same test, not traced there. Decision: the file `GitEnv::apply` names, the bench's own git's, carries `repack.updateServerInfo=false`; the agent's copy under `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` does not, so the fake harness's git still relies on the agent environment's override, as in production, and the check is unchanged. The runner's git in the bench reads the same file; its own `-c` stays pinned by `the_runners_git_leaves_the_server_info_alone`, which uses a configuration of its own. Not chosen: exempting `info/refs` from the check, even when its content matches the refs, for a write only the bench makes; the agent environment for each step the bench plays inside a run, where one step missed is the same flake and setup commands, whose detached maintenance can outlive them, stay uncovered; `maintenance.auto=false` in the bench, which removes more than the one write. Tests: `git::tests::the_benchs_git_leaves_the_server_info_alone`, where a `gc` through the bench's git writes no `info/refs` and one reading the agent's copy does; with the agent environment's entry removed, `a_runs_own_work_passes_and_every_breach_is_found` fails on `info/refs` (checked on 2026-10-12), so that test still guards the override.
- **Rebase runs and parallel runs** (OWL-49, decided 2026-09-28). The check takes two inputs the executor does not pass yet: the rebase role (P3) passes the first, the scheduler (P6) the second. Since OWL-210 the executor passes the first, for a brief with a `rebase`. A run alone still calls `Snapshot::take` and `Snapshot::check`, whose rules are unchanged.
  - *Rebase.* `Snapshot::take_rebase` names the new base, resolved to a commit when the snapshot is taken: remote-tracking refs are outside the ref check, so a name resolved after the run could be moved by the agent onto any history. After the run the branch must descend from its start, as for every run, or from that base. The start stays acceptable because a rebase that meets a conflict it cannot resolve aborts and escalates, leaving the branch as it was. Whether the run's commits were replayed is not checked: resolving a conflict changes a patch, a commit already upstream is dropped, a reword changes an id, so any such rule would quarantine good rebases. The replayed content is judged like any run's commits, by the gate, the verify stage and the review. Everything else is checked as for any run: `git rebase --update-refs` moves the branches stacked on the run's and is quarantined, so the rebase role runs without it.
  - *Parallel runs.* `Snapshot::check_among` asks the scheduler twice, just before and just after it reads the refs, for the other runs in flight at any time since the snapshot (`Concurrent`): the branches of runs still running, and those of runs that ended, each with the commit its own check verified (`Checked::tip`). A branch running in either answer is left out, its own run's check guarding it, and so is one that only the second answer knows or whose tip changed between the two. A branch that had already ended in the first answer must still be at its tip. The second answer keeps a run that starts during the check from being flagged; the first keeps a run that commits and ends after its branch was read, but before the second answer, from being held to a tip the read could not see. Tags, the stash, every other branch (a parked ticket's included), the shared git files and the main checkout are checked as for a run alone, and a breach there quarantines every run whose check sees it: the check cannot tell which run did it. The entry of a run in flight in `worktrees/` is left out as a running branch is (OWL-339), by the branch the entry belonged to when the snapshot was taken (`owner`), never by a later `HEAD` of an existing entry, which a run could forge. `owner` reads the entry's `HEAD` and its `rebase-merge/head-name` and `rebase-apply/head-name` in up to three rounds (the `head-name` files, `HEAD`, the `head-name` files again), because a run in flight rewrites them while they are read: a missing `HEAD` is an entry under creation, a symbolic `HEAD` names its branch, a detached one belongs to the branch a `head-name` names, and one with none is a rebase finishing between the reads, so the round is repeated, and after three rounds the entry is on no branch and never exempt. Each file is read as a file a run can write: a plain file, a few kilobytes of UTF-8 on one line, so a FIFO, a link or a huge file planted there blocks nothing, is not followed and gives no exemption (a `HEAD` that exists and is none of these reads as on no branch, never as under creation). An entry the snapshot did not see, or caught while `git worktree add` built it, is judged by its owner read after the run and after the second answer, which is what lets a run that registered between the two answers, with its entry made before the second, pass; nothing is read for a run alone (`Snapshot::check`, what production calls), which has no exemption. Limits: a brand-new entry planted whole with a `HEAD` naming a running branch (a write the sandbox refuses); the exemption itself, which hides every write to the entry of a run in flight, a FIFO, link or oversized file planted as its `HEAD` after the snapshot included, so the sandbox alone guards that entry; an entry in the middle of an `--apply` rebase, which reads as on no branch (kept as a limit, decided 2026-10-19, and pinned by `an_entry_in_the_middle_of_an_apply_rebase_reads_as_detached`; see the live checks, above); and a bare `git gc`, which rewrites the `logs/HEAD` of every entry (live check (d), above: git 2.54.0, 2026-10-19, a trailing tab on the first line) and so reads as a breach in a run that is not confined, where in the sandbox the write is refused. The detached maintenance a `fetch` or a `commit` starts (OWL-197) does it too once its reflog step runs (live check (e), above): by default when the repository's `HEAD` reflog holds 100 expired entries, under a `gc` strategy past `gc.auto`; past `gc.auto` the default maintenance of git 2.54.0 only repacks. Started by the runner's own `fetch` in `project::sync_checkout`, outside the sandbox, it would read as a breach of every run whose window it lands in, so the runner's git never expires reflogs or worktree entries in housekeeping (decided 2026-10-19, see live check (e)); a git outside the runner's, run by a process that is not confined, still can. `tests/isolation.rs` asserts that a `gc` by the agent's git leaves another entry alone, whose reflog holds an expired line, and that one expiring reflogs as git's defaults do is reported exactly as that entry's `logs/HEAD`.
  - *What P6 holds to.* The scheduler registers a run before creating its branch. When a run's check returns, the scheduler moves that branch to the ended runs with the check's tip, and nothing touches it until every run that overlapped it has been checked. A run's branch is held to its tip only by the checks that start after its own check returned; the ones already reading leave it out. A run whose check returned no tip is quarantined and stays out of both sets, so any change to its branch is flagged. Branches are created from a commit, as the executor does: made from a remote-tracking name, a branch gets `branch.<name>` entries in the shared configuration. Worktrees stay outside the main checkout, where a new one would show in its status. The runner's own git work for other runs during a run writes only their branches and remote-tracking refs: a fetch that follows tags would write `refs/tags/`.
  - *Its limit.* A run that appends a commit to another running run's branch, without breaking that run's history rule, goes unseen: while both run, that change cannot be told apart from the other run's own work.
- **Other worktrees' entries in the common git folder** (OWL-339, decided 2026-10-17 and 2026-10-18). Found by Codex in its review of OWL-329's pull request: a confined run could write the files of other worktrees under `.git/worktrees/<other ticket>/`, such as `index` or `HEAD`, and the isolation snapshot and check, after a run and after the gate, did not look at them, so a run could change what another ticket's worktree stages or sees without the breach firing. Decision (the decider's answer A, 2026-10-17): the entries are closed in the sandbox *and* checked after the run, so a run that gets past the sandbox, or a bare test run, is still marked as a breach. The approach and the rounds that settled its details follow.
  - *Sandbox.* `agent_env` protects `<git>/worktrees` whole and `Policy::reopened` writes the run's own entry again inside it (`RunPaths::worktree_git_dir`, derived by `own_entry`, the one derivation the check shares). On Seatbelt the reopening is an `allow file-write*` after the protected `deny file-write*` of the same operation; on bwrap a `--bind-try` of the entry deeper than the read-only bind of `worktrees`. The gate runs confined with the same paths (`project_gate` keeps the field). The cost: a run can no longer `git worktree add|remove|lock|prune`, and `git prune` and `git gc` cannot remove or rewrite another ticket's entry, even an expired one. What they do under a `worktrees/` made read-only to its files (`chmod -R a-w`, 2026-10-19, above) is recorded in the live checks: both end with status 0, saying `failed to delete` and `cannot lock ref`, and the expired entry's files are intact. That is plain git's; the agent's git, whose environment keeps housekeeping from expiring reflogs and entries (OWL-197 above), has `gc` try neither: checked on 2026-10-19 at 20:15 UTC on the same machine and git, on the (d) fixture with `worktrees/` read-only but the running worktree's own entry, as the sandbox leaves it, and the four settings given as the agent environment gives them (`GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`), `git gc --quiet` ended 0 with an empty standard error and left the other entries' files unchanged (`shasum`), and `git worktree prune --verbose` ended 0 with the `Removing` and `failed to delete` lines. The confined tests assert those statuses; the CI legs confirm them under the real sandboxes.
  - *Check.* `Violation::WorktreeEntries`, compared with the refs (so after the file-system checks of `check_files`, which do not read the entries). The walker keeps raw file names (a name that is not UTF-8 is a key of its own), reads a path that vanishes as absent, and compares a file past 1 MiB by its SHA-256 instead of its first MiB (decided 2026-10-18, round 3, Q1, A; this retires the first-MiB limit for the shared git files too). Hashing whole files is bounded by a budget of 1 GiB per read of the shared files and, per read of the entries, 1 GiB for each entry and 64 GiB for all of them, past which the read stops one byte later and the check reports `CheckFailed` (after the run or the gate) or, before it, the snapshot fails and the run does not start, as a review of the pull request asked: without it a file planted past the sandbox, sparse and as large as the file system allows, would keep the check reading with no deadline. The entries have a budget each because the runner removes no ticket worktree, so they add up with every ticket a clone has run, where one budget for all of them would end in a check that never passes (a later review of the pull request). The worked bound: an `index` takes about 100 bytes a tracked file, some 100 MiB for a million files, well within an entry's 1 GiB; whatever the repository, its checkouts take some 40 times what their indexes do (at least a 4 KiB block a file), so 64 GiB of indexes, some 650 of a million files, comes with 2.5 TiB of checkouts on the same disk. The total stays so that a run past the sandbox cannot add a GiB of reading with every entry it plants. Every file of an entry is kept as its size and digest, never its bytes, so that what the check holds in memory grows with the entries' files, not their bytes. Violations spell paths with `/` on every system (round 5, Q2, A). The exemption of a concurrent run's entry and its limits are under "Parallel runs" above (round 2, Q1, A; round 4, Q1, A; round 5, Q3, A; and the pass-6 decisions that a FIFO, link or oversized file planted after the snapshot in the entry of a run in flight stays a documented limit).
  - *Tests.* `executor::isolation`'s unit tests (the owner protocol with a hook that rewrites the entry between the reads, the plants, the exemption by the snapshot's owner, raw names), `owlshift-testkit/tests/isolation.rs` on a real second worktree (a run's commit, `add` and rebase pass; each write to another entry is found; the registration race), and `confinement.rs`, a confined run and a confined gate with their bare controls (round 5, Q1, A). CI requires confinement on the macOS leg too and prints the confinement tests' output (approved 2026-10-18, rounds 2 and 3, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.config/nextest.toml`). That a passing test's output is printed was doubted in a review of the pull request (nextest's `final-status-level`, whose default is not `pass`) and checked on 2026-10-19 with cargo-nextest 0.9.146, the version CI pins, on macOS: `cargo nextest run -p owlshift-testkit --locked --test confinement -E 'test(a_confined_run_cannot_write_another_worktrees_entry_but_its_own)'` ended 0 and printed, after its `Summary` line, the passing test's standard output and error, `bare run: prune=0 gc=0` and its `prune.out` and `gc.err` lines included; the profile needs no other setting.
  - *Live checks.* "OWL-339 live checks", above. The git order the owner protocol rests on holds for the merge backend; the `--apply` backend detaches `HEAD` before it writes any state, so its entry reads as on no branch. The Build could not nest `sandbox-exec` and has no `bwrap`: the sandbox's closing of the entries is designed and tested (the confined run and gate tests, which fail instead of skipping on both CI legs), but not yet observed on macOS or Linux, and the ticket is not done until the dated result of the pull request's CI legs (run ids, date, the `prune` and `gc` lines of the passing confinement tests) is added here, the last step of the ticket and not done by the Build.
- **Checked live on 2026-09-28** with Claude Code 2.1.283, `haiku` at effort `low`, the adapter's flags, under `env -i HOME PATH USER LANG TMPDIR`. With either permission mapping (read-only: `dontAsk` plus `Edit(./.owlshift/run/result.json)`; write in worktree: `acceptEdits` plus Bash), a role read the brief at `.owlshift/run/brief.json`, which git reports as ignored, and wrote `.owlshift/run/result.json`: exit status 0, no permission denial, `apiKeySource: none`, no MCP server. Then `crates/owlshift-testkit/tests/executor_live.rs`, run by hand with `OWLSHIFT_LIVE_CLAUDE=1` from inside a Claude Code session on the maintainer's machine, took a tiny read-only role through the executor: no credential finding, isolation held, `Finished` with the expected summary, in 9 s. C7's open item, the exit status at a real usage limit, stays open: no limit was reached.
- **Tests.** The scenarios run on the executor and the fake harness: the smoke scenario (a usage limit, a crash, questions, a valid build committed and pushed, an invalid result, a valid verify), and three short ones on its fixture in `crates/owlshift-testkit/tests/scenarios.rs`: a file written in the main checkout and a branch switch, both quarantined and parked despite a valid result, and a run stopped at a one-second deadline. `tests/isolation.rs` finds every kind of violation on one repository, after a run's own work (a commit, its ignored files, a fetched ref) passes. It passes a rebase onto the new base and an aborted one, and quarantines a rewrite below both the start and the base with the remote-tracking ref moved, and a rebase with `--update-refs` (git 2.54 locally, then CI's). Two concurrent runs pass, one ending first, while during a check a third starts and a fourth ends at a commit the check's read did not see; the same check told of no other run, an ended run's branch moved, a parked ticket's branch and a tag are still found; `tests/executor.rs` checks the refusals before the spawn: a credential in the repository's configuration, a worktree on another branch, a separate clone and the main checkout given as the worktree, run files reached through a link.

**The project gate** (OWL-16), in `owlshift-runner::executor::gate`. The build role checks its change with `stack.gate`'s commands and says so in its result, but a claim is never proof: after every Build run that ends `done` and breaks no isolation, the executor runs the whole gate itself. Since OWL-355 (below, "The role's checks") that run is the only full one of a Build.

- **How.** Each command of the brief's `gate`, in order, from the worktree root, through the platform's shell: `sh -c` on Unix, `cmd /d /s /c` on Windows with the line passed verbatim. It runs with the agent environment (OWL-22), never the runner's, with no input, as the root of a process tree; the first failure stops the gate. An empty gate runs nothing and passes, which the delivery report states as "the project declares no gate command". Since OWL-332 (below), no Build is dispatched on one, and the build role stops with `blocked` on one anyway, so only a rebase run, a role that ignored that rule or a test bench gets there.
- **An empty gate** (OWL-332, decided 2026-10-17). Found on Locary's LOC-1407 during OWL-309: with `gate = []` on the default branch, `doctor` said nothing blocked `do`, then the Build ran a whole plan and stopped `blocked`, as `roles/build.md` asks. Every dispatch but a rebase's now refuses an empty gate before its run: `OnDemand::dispatch` checks the gate it has just read at the configuring commit (OWL-331), before the `dispatch` event, for `do`, the drain's dispatch, `continue` and Watch's fix and correction runs (`Command::refuses_empty_gate`). A rebase still passes one, since its role does not stop on it. `continue` checks earlier, as soon as it reads a ticket at Design, Design review, Build or Verify, before it restarts a park or runs an answer check; it fast-forwards no branch, so its configuring commit is already the dispatch's. `do` checks in the dispatch alone, after its branch may have been fast-forwarded to the base. The refusal names the file and the commit read, and the fix: list the commands under `stack.gate` and merge it. A branch configured at an older commit keeps that commit's gate, so the refusal also says that `do` starts again from the base a branch with no commits of its own, and that one with commits of its own must first be rebased onto it. A refused fix run in `watch` leaves the ticket ref as it was, so watch holds it for its retry delay. `doctor` reports the same (above, "The project's gate"). Not chosen: refusing only `do` and the drain, the ticket's letter, which leaves `continue` and the fix runs spending a run that can only stop `blocked`; refusing intake, which runs no gate. Tests: `on_demand`'s `an_empty_gate_is_refused_by_every_dispatch_but_a_rebase`, and `tests/on_demand.rs`'s `an_empty_gate_refuses_do_before_any_run` and `an_empty_gate_refuses_continue_before_its_answer_check`.
- **On the delivered commit.** The worktree must be clean before the gate (uncommitted changes fail it without running a command), clean after it, and at the same HEAD: a passing gate vouches for the commit the run delivers, which the report records. These checks run git with the agent environment and `core.fsmonitor` off, so a helper the run planted in the repository's configuration never runs with the runner's environment, and their status with every configured filter driver switched off (OWL-73, under "Follow-ups"). The result and its artifacts are read before the gate, so gate code cannot change what the runner acts on.
- **Output and deadline.** The commands' standard output and standard error go interleaved, and whole unless abandoned, to `gate.log` in the caller's run directory; a failure keeps the last 16 KiB, cut on a character boundary. The commands share one deadline, `Executor::gate_timeout` (`DEFAULT_GATE_TIMEOUT`, 30 minutes, until a caller has a reason for another): each gets what is left, none starts once it is spent, and a command running at the deadline is stopped with its tree. The tree is also stopped when its root exits, so a process left holding the output does not hold the gate; the output of one that left the tree is waited for until the deadline, at least 2 s, then abandoned, and the gate fails with the output read until then (OWL-54), which is also what the log has of it. What it writes later is still read, so the process is not stopped by a closed pipe, but dropped: the drain writes the log under a lock that spans each write, and the gate empties it under that lock when it abandons the output, so nothing lands after the command's status line (OWL-69). The checks around the commands keep the executor's own 120 s git deadline.
- **Verdict.** Isolation is checked again after the gate, since the gate ran code the run wrote (the gate is confined as the run was, its own entry written and the other worktrees' entries closed, and the check reads those entries as after a run, OWL-339): a breach quarantines the run, whatever the gate said. Otherwise a red gate makes the run `Failure::Gate`, which the core counts as a failed run (`RunFailed`): the ticket stays at Build, nothing is pushed, and the existing breaker gives one fix run before parking it. The caller puts the failure in the next Build brief (`gate_failure`), where the build role reads its output as data; a passing gate clears it, and `RunReport::gate` carries the passing commands and commit for the delivery report. The failure is kept in memory by the caller until ticket refs persist a checkpoint.
- **The role's checks** (OWL-355, decided 2026-10-19). Found on OWL-160's drain run of 2026-10-18: its first Build took 85 minutes, a third of them spent by the build role waiting on the whole workspace suite, which the runner's gate ran once more right after `done`.
  - *Decision.* The build role no longer runs the whole gate before `done`. It runs each command of the brief's `gate` that bears on its change, narrowed by a scope that command's own tool offers (a package, a path, a test filter) and keeping its program, variable assignments and flags; a command with no narrower scope runs whole, and one that checks nothing the change can affect is left to the runner (`roles/build.md`, "The gate"). The whole gate is the runner's, run once after each `done` as before: a red one is a failed run and gives the next Build `gate_failure`, as before. A change that breaks a part its checks left out now costs a failed run against `MAX_FAILED_RUNS` rather than a role-side full run, so the role is told to widen its scope to the parts that use what it changed, once a run since OWL-366 (below, "Once a run"). Nothing changes in the runner, the brief or the result, and no format moves.
  - *Rejected: a `stack.quick_gate` list* in `owlshift.toml`, such as `["cargo fmt …", "cargo clippy …", "cargo test -p <crate>"]`. It needs a configuration key and a brief field, so a brief format bump; a placeholder such as `<crate>` that the runner would have to define for each language and tool; and a fixed list cannot name the crates a change touches, so it is either the whole gate again or a list the role still narrows. The trust rule is unchanged: the role narrows commands of `gate` on its own judgment, and no repository content adds one.
  - *This repository's gate runs under nextest.* `owlshift.toml` runs `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked --no-fail-fast` and `cargo test --workspace --doc --locked`, CI's two steps (OWL-241), in place of `cargo test --workspace --locked`, and `the_repositorys_own_project_file_gates_on_what_ci_runs` requires both by name. A machine that runs Owlshift on this repository needs cargo-nextest on the `PATH` its agents get. CI's Windows leg leaves on_demand out of its nextest step; the gate does not, since only macOS and Linux run it.
  - *Measured on 2026-10-19.* The run directories are the maintainer's, under the data directory's `projects/github.com/pitchopp/owlshift/runs/owl-160/`.
    - OWL-160's first Build, before (`20261018T203335Z`): brief written at 20:33 UTC, the agent's last output at 21:48, `gate.log` at 21:58: 75 minutes of agent, then 10 minutes of the runner's gate, 85 in all. The agent's `tool_progress` heartbeats, every 30 s and so lower bounds, show four waits on the whole suite of at least 480, 420, 570 and 540 s, 33 minutes. Each of the two Verify fix Builds that followed ran it once more on the role's side: at least 540 s of a 14-minute run (`20261018T220354Z`) and 570 s of a 10-minute run (`20261018T223317Z`), each followed by the runner's 9 to 10 minutes.
    - Where the gate's time goes. In the first Build's `gate.log` the test binaries ran 563 s, 524 s of them `tests/on_demand.rs`, whose 211 tests the `RUNS` mutex runs one after the other in one process under `cargo test`. The workspace was already built (`Finished … in 0.08s`), so the formatter, lint, rustdoc and the links of the test binaries took at most about 35 s of the 10 minutes: the sandbox's `xcrun_db` "Operation not permitted" warnings (246 lines) do not measurably slow the link.
    - Locally, unconfined and warm, on `aa2644c`: `cargo test --workspace --locked` 582 s; `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked --no-fail-fast` 117 s (1051 tests), and the doctests 2 s.
    - In the sandbox, through `owlshift gate --at` on this change's branch (OWL-329), the way a Build's gate runs, from the maintainer's checkout with no drain running, on a warm target: the gate passed on `af3026a` in 182.7 s, the formatter 0.7 s, lint 12.5 s, rustdoc 5.4 s, nextest 157.4 s (its test run 127.5 s, 1053 tests) and the doctests 1.3 s. Under `cargo test`, OWL-160's runner gate took about 10 minutes, its test run 563 s: the runner's gate is now about 3 minutes, and nextest needs no exception from the sandbox.
    - *Replayed live on 2026-10-19* (OWL-360), once. OWL-160's first Build ran again on `main` at `4f3973a` through the runner's executor, wired as `owlshift do` wires a Build: confined, on the token for agent runs, with `roles/build.md` and this repository's nextest gate, on `claude-sonnet-5-5` at effort high as before, from a warmed target, and with no tracker and no forge, so nothing was posted or pushed. Its brief is the first Build's as `main` now renders it, rebuilt by OWL-359's method with the current gate: 88,974 bytes against the before's 384,057, so the comparison mixes the smaller brief with the scoped checks. It ended `done`, eleven commits and a passing gate: 68.1 minutes of agent, then 2.4 minutes of the runner's gate (`gate.log` from its creation to its last write; nextest 143 s, 1132 tests), 70.5 in all, against 75.3 and 9.4 by the same method, 85; 12.90 USD and 372 turns, against 9.70 USD and 240.
    - The runner's gate ran once, as `Executor::run` runs it after each `done`. The role did not leave the whole suite out: of its 67 cargo calls, seven were `cargo nextest run --workspace --locked --no-fail-fast`, most started in the background and polled, whose own summaries add up to 25 minutes (128 to 388 s each), and one ran the doctests. Its heartbeat waits, lower bounds, add up to at least 22.5 minutes, against the before's 44. The change touches four of the seven crates, core and runner among them, which the CLI and the testkit use, so "the parts that use what you changed" reach nearly the whole workspace: for such a change, the role's instruction does not keep it off the whole suite. Of the 14 minutes saved, 7 are the gate's and 7 the agent's. `main` had moved since the before (#310 to #318) and a model's run varies from one run to the next, so these times are one reading, not a bound.
    - *Still projected:* each Verify fix Build would wait about 3 minutes, not 10, for the runner's gate; none was replayed. The replay's logs were kept in a session's scratch folder, not in the data directory.
  - *What the bench shows.* `tests/scenarios/gate_scope.toml` replays a Build whose change to one part breaks a check of another that its own check left out: the runner's gate, run once (`gate_log`), fails on the other part's command, the retry gets that command in `gate_failure`, and the gate passes after it. A scripted result cannot show what a real model runs: whether the role leaves the whole suite out is checked live only, by the replay of OWL-160's first Build above.
  - *Once a run* (OWL-366, decided 2026-10-19). The replay above ran the whole suite seven times in the role.
    - *Where the seven fell.* From the replay's `stdout.log`, its commands in order: six inside four of its eleven steps, two each in the steps naming the harness and its stops and recording the stop in the ticket ref, one each in the resume through `continue` and the resume in watch; the seventh after the last commit. Their summaries, in order: 2 of 1094 tests failed, then 1098 passed; 1 of 1098 failed, then 1105 passed; 4 of 1114 failed; 3 of 1128 failed; 1132 passed. Every failure was an existing test of the testkit or the CLI, parts that use what the steps changed: `an_empty_gate_refuses_continue_before_its_answer_check` among the first two, four `doctor` tests in the 1114. The log shows commands, not reasons: the likeliest reading is that each step widened its checks to what uses its change, nearly the whole workspace from core and the runner, and each fix ran them again, as "a change after them means running them again" asked.
    - *Decision.* A step checks what it changed (its own parts, and by a test filter the tests it wrote), not what uses it. Once, before `done`, the role widens to the parts that use what its plan's steps changed, running a command whole when they are most of what it checks. That wider check runs once in a run: a fix of what it finds is a step of its own, after which only the part that failed and what the fix touches run again. A command that offers a narrower scope runs whole once in a run at most; one that offers none runs whole, as before (`roles/build.md`, "The gate"). "Before `done`" and "your plan's steps" cover a resumed run whose steps an earlier run committed.
    - *What it costs.* Applied to the replay's commands, the rule keeps one whole-suite run of seven, the last, which passed; the failures the in-step runs found would surface there instead, fixed in a step and re-run narrowed. A broad change that passes pays that run twice, in the role and in the runner's gate (128 to 388 s each in the replay): the wider check of a change that reaches most of the workspace can amount to every command of the gate, which "Do not run the whole gate yourself as a last check" leaves allowed, being the check of what uses the change rather than a last check of everything; for any other change, the runner's run stays the only full one. Left to the runner's gate, as a failed run: a part broken by a fix made after the wider check, which is checked narrowed only, and a step that changes most parts directly, whose own checks are near whole without being whole. Each new run, a fix run or one told of a `gate_failure` included, widens once again.
    - *Rejected: leaving the wider check to the runner's gate past a breadth threshold.* It saves the duplicate, but a broad change is the one most likely to break a part that uses it, as four of the replay's six in-step runs found: each break would cost a failed run against `MAX_FAILED_RUNS` (2) and a fix Build, likely some ten minutes at the length of OWL-160's Verify fix Builds, and a second failure parks the ticket. Rejected too: per-step widening capped at one whole run a step, still one run a step on a broad change.
    - *Evidence.* `build_widens_its_checks_once` (`owlshift-contracts/tests/roles.rs`) pins the rule and the absence of the per-step widening; the edit rewrites one line of `roles/build.md`, within `BUILD_MAX_LINES`. No replay was run for this change: the maintainer took the role test and the projection above as its evidence on 2026-10-19, and the next real Build of a broad change is the live reading.
- **Tests.** Unit tests in `gate.rs` run real commands on the three CI platforms: order and first failure, the tail of a long output, the deadline, a process left holding the output, the tail taken while the output is still open, an abandoned drain's log emptied only once its write in progress landed and written no more (OWL-69), the agent environment, and on Windows a line with inner quotes; on Unix, a process that left the tree and holds the output fails the gate with the output read before, and nothing it writes after the gate returned reaches the log, which ends with the lines of a command run next on it (OWL-69), and attributes naming a driver of the repository's configuration and one of the user's run neither in the gate's git, before or after the commands, while a plain `git status` runs both (OWL-73). The scenario `tests/scenarios/gate.toml` is the acceptance: the gate fails after a first Build `done`, the next brief carries the failure, and it passes after the fix run. `tests/scenarios/gate_scope.toml` is OWL-355's (above). Short scenarios on its fixture cover a gate refused on uncommitted changes, a gate that commits, and a gate that breaks isolation and fails, which is quarantined.
- **Follow-ups.** A gate-specific attempt counter, if one fix run proves too few: decided against on 2026-10-10 (OWL-208, answering OWL-55). The one red gate of the P1 and P2 deliveries (OWL-174's timing flake, OWL-177) passed at its single retry, so the gate keeps `MAX_FAILED_RUNS`; what P3 bounds instead is the fix passes after delivery ("Watch after delivery", above). The isolation check's own `git status` still runs with the runner's environment, now only once the link and the shared git files are found untouched (OWL-59). The gate's own checks around its commands run git in the worktree with the agent environment, no hook and no monitor, outside the sandbox; OWL-64 makes the executor's file-system checks (the link, the shared git files) before that git, before the commands and again after them, and a violation stops the gate there and quarantines the run, so a clean filter the gate's code configured never runs; the gate's status ignores submodules, so a planted gitlink starts no git in an unchecked repository. One limit remains: a process that escaped the gate's tree could change those files after the check.
  - *Configured filter drivers* (OWL-73, decided 2026-09-29). The worktree's attributes, written by the run, choose the filter driver a file goes through, and a driver already configured (a global or system git-lfs, one in the repository's own configuration, such as a script in the worktree) would run in the gate's status outside the sandbox, on content the run chose. Just before each of the gate's two status calls, the gate lists every driver git would find (`git config -z --get-regexp '^filter\.'`, with the same environment and hardening as the status) and switches each off on that status: `-c filter.<name>.clean=`, `smudge=`, `process=` and `required=false`. This departs from the ticket's two options. Running the gate's git without the global and system configuration leaves the repository's own drivers running and loses the global `core.excludesFile`, so the gate would fail on files the user ignores. Recording the drivers in the snapshot and failing closed when attributes name one would fail every gate of a repository that uses git-lfs, whose committed attributes name it; `merge.*.driver` never runs in a status. Two costs: a file a driver filters is read raw, so once its timestamps change (touched, or racily clean after a checkout or a commit) it reads as modified and the gate fails, which a repository using git-lfs should expect, the failure naming the drivers switched off; and a driver whose name a `-c` argument cannot carry (holding `=`, empty, not UTF-8) fails every gate, as does a listing git could not give. Checked on 2026-09-29 with git 2.54.0, a throwaway global configuration and a marker per driver: the gate's status and `rev-parse` ran no `diff.*.textconv`, `diff.*.command` or `merge.*.driver`, while `git diff` ran the diff command; the empty values stopped a clean driver and a `process` driver, whereas keeping `required=true` made the status die ("clean filter 'X' failed"); `-c filter.a=b.clean=` split at the first `=` and the `a=b` driver still ran; the listing gave every scope's keys, a name's case kept. A possible follow-up covers every program git could start at once: running the gate's own git inside the sandbox, where a driver still runs, but confined.

**A refused result** (OWL-180, decided 2026-10-07). On the P2 exit gate (OWL-175, below), a Build run asking after a round with a re-ask went on from the thread's numbering and asked `Q4`; the runner refused its `result.json` ("question Q4 is out of order: expected Q1"), its retry, told nothing, did the same, and the second failed run parked the ticket.

- **The role.** `roles/build.md` says that every run that asks numbers its questions from `Q1`, whatever ids the thread's earlier rounds and re-asks used.
- **The retry is told why.** A Build run whose `result.json` is refused (`Failure::InvalidResult`) is a failed run, as before, and the next Build run of the same command gets the reason in its brief (`result_refusal`, brief format 7; `result.json` stays format 5), as a red gate's next run gets `gate_failure`. `on_demand::result_refusal` reads it from the run's outcome for `OnDemand::build` and for the scenarios' stand-in driver alike: any other outcome leaves the next brief without it, so it reaches one run only, and never the answer check or the resolver (OWL-184, below, tells the answer check its own). The reason is the runner's message, cut to 2 KiB (`MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`) with its beginning kept, since it can quote a whole string of the refused result; what it quotes is data. A result holding the harness's login is a credential failure, checked before the contract (`executor::read_result`), so the reason never quotes it.
- **What it did not change.** The failed-run breaker: a refused run counted, so after another failed run at the same stage the refusal parked the ticket with no retry left to tell; OWL-183, below, keeps it from spending that last attempt. The reason lived in the command's memory, like `gate_failure`: a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped it, until OWL-192 kept it in the ticket ref ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below). The answer check is retried across commands, so carrying its refusals needs the ticket ref: OWL-184, below, does it, and records why the resolver gets none.
- **Rejected: the runner renumbering the questions itself.** The numbering carries nothing but order, but renumbering would loosen a strict contract for one shape only, while the brief's reason serves every refusal a run can fix by itself (an unknown field, `pr` without `done`, a wrong `pr.branch`). And a run's own text can cite its ids, in a question's `context` or a decision's `basis`, which a silent renumbering would leave pointing at another question.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/numbering.toml` is the acceptance: after a round with a re-ask, a run asking `Q3` is refused with nothing posted, and its retry, told why, opens round 2 with `Q1`. `a_refused_result_is_told_to_the_next_build_run_only` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop: the refusal reaches the next Build brief only, not the resolver's nor the Build run after, and `run_started` records it.

**A refused result at the last attempt** (OWL-183, decided 2026-10-07). With OWL-180 alone, a refused Build result was one more failed run: when another run of the stage had already failed, it was the second and parked the ticket, and its reason reached no run. A failed answer check counts toward the stage it returns to, so a round whose check failed once, followed by a resumed Build run that numbers on from the thread as OWL-175's did, parked with that fix unused.

- **The rule.** A Build run whose `result.json` is refused, and whose brief carried no `result_refusal`, counts as a failed run, except that it never brings the count to the breaker's limit: at the stage's last attempt, the ticket stays at Build with its count, nothing is posted, and the next Build run of the command gets that attempt, told why. A Build run that was told and fails, however, counts as any failed run, so at the last attempt it parks. Below the last attempt nothing changes: the first refusal of a ticket with no failed run counts, and its told retry is the last attempt.
- **Where.** The core event `Event::ResultRefused`, valid at Build, since only Build's brief carried a refusal's reason, and since OWL-190 while waiting for input too, where the answer check runs ("A refused answer check at the last attempt" below): it counts as `RunFailed` below the last attempt and leaves the state as it is at it, so `state.json` keeps its format and its invariants. The runner chooses it: `on_demand::stage_event` maps a Build run whose outcome `result_refusal` tells to it when the run's brief carried no `result_refusal` (`on_demand::freed_refusal`), and every other outcome as `core_event` does, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike. It covers every refusal `result_refusal` tells, the contract's and a result that is a link or past 1 MiB alike, and since OWL-184 an artifact the run named wrongly.
- **The bound.** Two refusals in a row never both go free: the second run was told. Each freed refusal is followed by a told run that parks, stops (a round for the decider, a usage limit), delivers, or goes on through a resolver's settled pass or a late-comment integration run; neither count is reset in a command (`MAX_RESOLVED_PASSES`, one integration per delivery), so a command frees a handful of refusals at most, each at the cost of one Build run. Across commands Build's reason was dropped, as OWL-180 said, so a `continue` after a usage limit could free a refusal again; since OWL-192 the ticket ref keeps it, the Build run of a later command is told, and its refusal counts, so the bound holds across commands too. The PARKED comment says "a run failed again, with no attempt left", true whether or not a freed refusal came before. The answer check's freed refusals have a bound of their own (OWL-190, below).
- **Rejected.** Counting as before and documenting the park: OWL-180's fix would never reach the case it was built for. A per-stage flag in `state.json` (format 3), set once a refusal was freed: the reason it pairs with lived in the command's memory, so the flag would outlive what it is for (OWL-192 keeps the reason in `questions.json`, and the told brief is the flag). A refusal that never counts: two in a row, and then more, would loop on paid runs. The rule in the runner alone, skipping the core event: the breakers live in the core machine, and the stand-in driver would need a copy. Freeing the contract's refusals only, not a link or a size: it would split `Failure::InvalidResult` for no case seen. The same rule frees a refused artifact once OWL-184 tells it: what is told is what is freed.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/last_attempt.toml` is the acceptance: a crashed answer check counts at Build, the resumed Build run asks `Q2` and is refused with nothing posted or parked, and its retry, told why, opens round 2 with `Q1`. `a_refused_result_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop and the bound: after a run with no result, a refused run does not park, and its told retry, refused again, does. `a_refused_result_counts_but_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`owlshift-core`) and the transition table pin the event.

**A refused answer check** (OWL-184, decided 2026-10-07). OWL-180 told only the next Build run of a command why its predecessor's `result.json` was refused. The answer check is retried too, by a later `continue` or watch, and a refused artifact (a file a result names in `artifacts`) was told to no run; either could repeat the same fixable mistake.

- **The answer check is told.** A check whose `result.json` is refused is a failed run, as before, and the next check on the same ask gets the reason in its brief's `result_refusal` (brief format 8, which allows the field for `build` and `answer_check`, and still refuses it for the resolver). Its retry is always a later command, so the reason is kept in the ticket ref, on the latest ask of `questions.json` (`Ask::result_refusal`, format 5), where the latest check's verdicts already live: a re-ask starts a new ask, so a reason never outlives the ask its check judged. `answer_check::keep`, for `OnDemand::check_answers` and the stand-in driver alike, keeps it with the verdicts, before the state moves, so a park keeps it too: a refused result sets it, any other kept outcome clears it (verdicts, a failed or crashed check, a quarantine), and a usage limit keeps nothing, so the next check is told what the interrupted one was. `TicketQuestions::validate` refuses a kept reason that is blank or past `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`.
- **At the last attempt.** A refused check first counted as any failed check, so after another failed run at the stage it parked; the reason stayed in the ref through the park, and the person's `continue` that restarted the ticket ran a told check. OWL-190 frees it as OWL-183 frees Build's ("A refused answer check at the last attempt" below).
- **The resolver was not told.** A refused resolver result is never retried as such: `resolver::outcome` makes it a fallback, and every question goes to the decider in a round that says why; a refusal costs the decider a question, never a loop. One case replays the same questions: a fallback round whose post or keeping failed, after which a later command's Build run may ask them again and the resolver run again. OWL-191 tells that run ("A refused resolver result told to its replay" below).
- **Refused artifacts are told.** `executor::Failure::refusal` says which failures are the run's own to fix: a refused `result.json`, and an artifact refused for its path or its file (`ArtifactError::is_the_runs`: a path that is not plain, a link, a second name, not a regular file, missing, too large). An artifact deleted while the runner read it, a worktree the runner cannot use, or a failing file system is not the run's mistake and is not told. The reason is the runner's message, such as "artifact `plan`: `.owlshift/run/plan.md` does not exist". It reaches Build's next run in the command and the answer check's next check alike, and OWL-183's freeing covers it since `stage_event` frees what `result_refusal` tells, as OWL-190's covers the answer check's.
- **Rejected.** The reason in `state.json`: the core state keeps no text, and OWL-183 already turned down a per-stage flag there. A top-level field of `questions.json`: every new ask would have to clear it. A third file in the ticket ref's tree: a format of its own for one string.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/check_refusal.toml` is the acceptance: round 1 asks Q1, a check giving its verdict on Q2 is refused with nothing posted (a counted `result_refused` since OWL-190, no run having failed before it), the next check stops at a usage limit, told why, and the one after it, told the same, answers the round. `a_refused_check_is_told_to_the_next_check_across_commands` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the ticket ref across commands and a park: after a crashed check, a refused one keeps its reason without parking (OWL-190), its told retry, refused again, parks keeping the new reason, and the restart's check is told and clears it. `a_refused_artifact_is_told_and_never_spends_the_last_attempt` checks a Build result naming a missing plan after a failed run. The contracts tests pin the brief's role check, the format-4 read and the kept reason's bounds; `on_demand`'s unit test which artifact refusals are told.

**A refused answer check at the last attempt** (OWL-190, decided 2026-10-08). With OWL-184 alone, a refused answer-check result was one more failed run: after a crashed check, the refusal parked the ticket, posted a PARKED comment, and took it out of watch's reach until a person typed `continue`, though the runner already knew what to tell the next check.

- **The rule.** OWL-183's, for the answer check: a check whose `result.json` (or an artifact it names) is refused, and whose brief carried no `result_refusal`, counts as a failed run, except that it never brings the count to the breaker's limit. At the last attempt the ticket keeps waiting for input with its count, nothing is posted, the latest ask keeps the reason (OWL-184), and the command stops as for any failed check (`Stop::CheckFailed`). A check that was told and fails counts as any failed run, so at the last attempt it parks. Below the last attempt nothing changes.
- **Where.** `Event::ResultRefused`, valid while waiting for input as at Build, so the breakers stay in the core machine and `state.json` keeps its format. `answer_check::event` takes the check's brief and maps a refusal to it when `on_demand::freed_refusal` says so, the test `stage_event` makes for Build, for `OnDemand::check_answers` and the stand-in driver alike; `answer_check::keep` keeps the reason for it as for a counted refusal.
- **When the retry runs.** The next check, as for any failed check: its retry is always a later command (OWL-184). Watch runs it at its next pass, since the kept reason moves the ticket ref and the 10-minute hold applies only to a continue that left the ref as it was; a person typing `continue` runs it too. Each such pass costs one check, until the told one gives its verdicts, fails or parks.
- **The bound.** Two refusals in a row never both go free: the reason is kept in the ticket ref, so the next check is told whatever command runs it, through a usage limit or a restart, and its refusal counts. A check that gives its verdicts clears the reason; a counter-question, which moves no counter, or a re-ask, which starts a new ask, can therefore be followed by another freed refusal. Each such cycle needs a new comment of the decider before a check runs again, so the runner never loops alone; the re-ask limit bounds the re-asks, and nothing bounds counter-questions but the decider, as before.
- **Rejected.** Retrying in the same command, `check_answers` running a told check right away: a second paid run in one command and a loop in the check's path and the stand-in driver, for a retry watch already makes at its next pass. Leaving it as OWL-184 left it: the park cost a PARKED comment on the ticket and a person's gesture for a mistake the runner can tell.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/check_last_attempt.toml` is the acceptance: round 1 asks Q1, the first check crashes, the second gives its verdict on Q2 and is refused with nothing posted or parked, and the third, told why, answers the round. `watch_retries_a_refused_check_at_the_last_attempt` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) plays it under watch, the told retry at the next pass, with no PARKED comment and no `continue` typed; `a_refused_check_is_told_to_the_next_check_across_commands` checks the bound across commands. `a_refused_check_is_freed_unless_it_was_told` (`answer_check`), `a_refused_result_counts_but_never_spends_the_last_attempt` (`owlshift-core`) and the transition table pin the event.

**A retry after refused decisions asks** (OWL-186, decided 2026-10-07). OWL-176 refused a Build result that lists `decisions` and told the next Build run of the command why, but nothing checked that run: one that dropped the refused decisions and reported `done` delivered the choices unasked, the silent settling OWL-176 set out to stop.

- **The rule.** The Build run told that its predecessor's decisions were refused may not end `done`: `RunResult::validate_against` refuses its `done`, whatever else it lists, and lets every other status through (`questions` asks; `blocked`, `premise_false` and `failed` stop without settling anything). The rule bounds `done` only: it does not check that each refused choice is asked, which OWL-193 does ("A retry asks every refused choice" below).
- **The kind.** The runner knows the refusal was of decisions from a typed flag, not from the reason's text. `RunResult::parse_against` checks the build role's decisions right after the document's shape, before every other rule, and says so (`result::Refused::decisions`, since OWL-195 the kind `result::DecisionsRefusal`: `Listed` or `Done`), so a result that lists decisions is refused for them whatever else it breaks; the executor makes that refusal `Failure::Decisions`, which carries the kind, told like `Failure::InvalidResult` and so freed at the last attempt by OWL-183; `on_demand::decisions_kind` reads it, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike, and the next Build brief of the command carries `decisions_refused` (brief format 9: in Build's brief only, beside its `result_refusal`; `result.json` stays format 6). The refusal of a `done` under the flag is a decisions refusal too, since the choices are still open; while `MAX_FAILED_RUNS` is 2 that reaches no run, the told run's refusal parking the ticket.
- **The retry knows what to ask.** The refused result is removed before the next run, so the decisions reason now quotes the choices it listed, each question with what was recorded, after the advice and within `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES` (a long list is cut, its beginning kept). A told run that lists decisions again gets a short advice before its choices (OWL-195): its list is told after the first one, whose full advice comes first ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below).
- **The role.** `roles/build.md`: with `decisions_refused`, ask each refused choice, even one found settled since by the decider's word, a `decision` entry, a rule or the repository, its `context` naming what settles it (the one exception to never asking again what the decider's word settles), and end with any status but `done`.
- **False refusals.** A retry that finds a refused choice settled since (the decider commented between the two runs, or the refused run listed as a decision what only followed the thread, a rule or the repository) still may not end `done`: it asks. Since OWL-222 such a question goes to the decider, never to the resolver, so it costs the decider a question, its `context` naming what settles it. That is a question, never a wrong decision, and the refused run had ignored the prompt first. A retry that ends `done` anyway is a told run's refusal, a failed run that parks the ticket (OWL-183's bound), and its PARKED comment quotes the reason to the person who restarts it.
- **The live run.** The ticket asked to weigh the rule against OWL-176's live acceptance run first; none is recorded (OWL-176's acceptance is `open_points.toml`, and the live runs this document records, the P2 exit gate's, came before it), and none could be made when this was decided. The rule does not rest on one: a retry that asks as told never meets it, so a live run showing retries that ask would make it costless, not unneeded, and OWL-176 rejected the prompt alone because nothing outside the run checks it.
- **What remains.** A told retry that asks some refused choices and drops others went unseen, the rule reading the status, until OWL-193 (below). The flag lived in the command's memory with the reason (OWL-180), so a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped it and the first Build run of a later command was untold; OWL-192 keeps both in the ticket ref ("A Build refusal kept across commands" below). A result whose shape is broken (an unknown field, a wrong type) is refused for it before its decisions are read. Choices never recorded stay unseen (OWL-176).
- **Rejected.** No rule, the design recording why: OWL-176's reason for refusing the prompt alone holds. Reading the kind from the reason's text: the reason is the runner's free message, reworded at will and able to quote the refused result, where a typed flag is checked by `Brief::validate`. Letting a `done` through when the thread changed since the refused run: a comment that settles nothing would unlock it, and the runner cannot tell which comment settles a choice. Requiring as many questions as refused decisions: a run may merge or split choices, or find one followed, so the count would refuse good retries and pass bad ones. The runner asking the refused decisions itself: a decision carries neither the category the routing needs nor a question's context, and the decider would get the run's wording of a choice already built.
- **Tests.** `tests/scenarios/open_points_unasked.toml` is the acceptance, on `open_points`' fixture: the first Build run decides both points and is refused; its retry, told why, settles one in a commit the gate would pass and reports `done`; that result is refused before the gate, nothing is pushed, and the ticket parks with a PARKED comment quoting the reason (without the rule, the retry is delivered). `open_points.toml` keeps the retry that asks. `a_refused_result_is_told_to_the_next_build_run_only` (`tests/on_demand.rs`) checks the real loop: a refusal is told to the next Build brief only, not the resolver's nor the Build run after, and a decisions refusal flags that brief, whose question then goes to the decider (OWL-222). `decisions_against_the_brief` (`owlshift-contracts`) pins the rule under every status, the quoted choices, and decisions refused before a misnumbered question; the executor's unit test and `on_demand`'s pin the failure's kind and that it is told.

**A Build refusal kept across commands** (OWL-192, decided 2026-10-08). OWL-180's reason and OWL-186's flag lived in the command's memory: after a refusal of decisions, a park, a usage limit or the end of the command dropped them, and the first Build run of a later command (`continue`, watch's continue, `do`) was told neither why nor that it may not end `done`, so the refused choices could be settled silently. The hold now outlives the command.

- **Where.** In the ticket ref, as a top-level field of `questions.json` (`TicketQuestions::build_refusal`, format 6): since format 8 (OWL-195, decided 2026-10-08), the reason of every refusal that listed choices since no Build run asked, oldest first (`lists`, which hold the next runs while not empty), and the latest refusal's reason when it listed none (`reason`, absent when the latest is the last list). Format 6 and 7 kept the hold as a flag (`decisions`), the latest reason and, once a later refusal took its place, the hold's first list (`decisions_reason`); each is read through its own shape and upgraded: the held reason becomes the first list and the latest reason, of a kind those formats did not keep, a list after it, so no later refusal drops it, told where it was; a hold without a held reason becomes one list. Since format 9 (OWL-198, decided 2026-10-08), a refusal that lists choices keeps them typed (`choices`, `result::RefusedChoice`: each decision's question and what it recorded, its basis left out, each text cut to `MAX_CHOICE_BYTES`, 512 bytes, its beginning kept), carried from `RunResult::parse_against` in the refusal's kind (`DecisionsRefusal::Listed`), at most `MAX_REFUSED_CHOICES`, 64, those past it counted (`left_out`); `lists` holds only what formats 6 to 8 kept as text, and a format-8 document is read with its lists as they were. OWL-184 kept the answer check's on its ask, and turned down a top-level field because every new ask would have to clear it; a Build refusal belongs to no ask, and a new ask must not clear it, so the top level is its place. `state.json` keeps no text (OWL-183, OWL-184). `TicketQuestions::validate` refuses the reason or a list blank or past `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES`, more than 64 choices or a choice's text past 512 bytes, choices counted as left out while there is room for them, and a refusal that keeps neither a reason nor a choice.
- **When.** `on_demand::build_refusal_after`, for `OnDemand::build` and the stand-in driver alike, after each Build run that did not break isolation: a refusal `result_refusal` tells takes the kept reason's place, except that one that lists choices, as the failure's kind says (`DecisionsRefusal::Listed`, never the reason's text), is added to the lists (since OWL-198, its choices to the kept ones, `BuildRefusal::keep_choices`), and every later refusal keeps them, so a later refusal for another fault (a broken shape, a refused `done`) or another list never drops one; a reason that lists none is kept only while it is the latest, holding no choice; an accepted result (a valid `result.json`, or a `done` whose gate failed) clears a refusal of anything but decisions; a hold is cleared only by a result that asks, `questions` or `premise_false` (since OWL-193, one with a question), since a `blocked` or `failed` result asked nothing and a `done` is refused under it; a usage limit, a run that failed with nothing told and a quarantine leave it, so the next run is told the same. A told run that crashed no longer drops the flag for the next run of the same command either.
- **What the run is told.** Every Build brief, whatever the command, carries the kept reason in `result_refusal` and the hold in `decisions_refused` (`on_demand::tell_build`, `BuildRefusal::told`): every list, oldest first, then the latest reason when it listed none, each after "A later result was refused too:", within `MAX_RESULT_REFUSAL_BYTES` (OWL-195). OWL-194 told the hold's first list and the latest reason only, so from the third refusal a list between them was not told, and cut the whole text with its beginning kept, so a long first list cut the later ones off. Items that do not fit whole now share the room: one shorter than its share stays whole, the others are cut to it, each keeping its beginning and marked `[cut]`. A refusal's choices come after its advice, so the share must leave room for them: a told run that lists decisions again gets a short advice (`RunResult::parse_against` reads the brief's `decisions_refused`), its list always following the first one, whose full advice is told first; three long lists and a refused `done` then each tell the start of their choices. Past ten items, the most that fit at 160 bytes each, the first list (and its advice) and the eight newest items are told, a note counting the lists left out between them. That sharing still cut choices: the end of a long list, the lists between, and a list past 2 KiB already when kept. **Since OWL-198 the choices are told typed, in a field of their own, rather than in a larger `result_refusal`:** Build's brief carries `refused_choices` (brief format 11), every kept choice, oldest first, so every choice reaches the run that asks, in one round for the decider; `result_refusal` keeps its 2 KiB cap for the runner's text, and tells the first list's full advice once, saying the choices are in `refused_choices` and how many were not kept, told whole, then the lists formats 6 to 8 kept as text and the latest reason, sharing the rest of the room as above (`BuildRefusal::told`). This reverses OWL-195's refusal of a brief format change ("Rejected" below): that change was a larger cap, which would still cut a long enough text, for a case that takes a person's restart at each step; a typed field ends the cutting of choices, keeps them apart from the advice they shared the room with, and gives OWL-193 a list to check a retry against, which a cap does not. "The refused choices" for a told run, OWL-193's check included, are those of `refused_choices`, plus those the lists formats 6 to 8 kept quote; a choice's place in `refused_choices`, from 1, stays the same for every run the hold tells, the list only growing until it clears, and is what such a check can match (OWL-193, below, defines the matching; text lists have no place, OWL-204 below says why they are left). A brief that carries a reason is told, so `stage_event` never frees its run's refusal (OWL-183), in a later command too.
- **Written when.** A refusal is written right after its run, before the state moves, so a park keeps it, and makes the ticket ref of a ticket that had none, a third exception to "makes no ticket ref until a round opens" after OWL-138's kept decision: `continue` then runs Build at Build, a PARKED comment says `continue`, and `do` starts from Ready keeping it. A clear is never written alone: a result that asks clears it with what keeps its questions, the round's ask or the resolver's first kept decision, so a QUESTIONS post that fails, or a resolver that breaks isolation, leaves it kept; any other clear goes with the next state kept. A write that fails stops the command. Watch continues through `OnDemand::continue_seen`, the same `continue` path.
- **What remains.** Under a hold the ticket cannot deliver until a Build run asks: a person who wants it delivered with a choice settled answers on the ticket and restarts it, and the run asks anyway, the decider confirming what the thread settles (OWL-186's false refusals, OWL-222); `owlshift forget` keeps the hold with the round count (OWL-202), and deleting the dedicated checkout drops it with every ticket's record. A run that asks some refused choices and drops others is refused since OWL-193 (below). Since OWL-198 every kept choice is told, within two bounds: a text past 512 bytes is told by its beginning, marked `[cut]`, and the role asks it as told; and a hold keeps 64 choices, the run being told how many more were listed and to ask about every choice its branch's commits took without the decider, since past 64 a run is not asking but dumping, and the ticket ref must stay readable (64 choices of two 512-byte texts stay far within its 1 MiB read cap, which `ticket_ref::write` does not check, even escaped as JSON). A choice listed again is kept again, as listed. Lists formats 6 to 8 kept as text still share the room as OWL-195 told them. Each told refusal costs a failed run and parks the ticket by the second, so a list past the first two needs a person's restart. `gate_failure` stays in the command's memory.
- **Rejected.** Clearing on any accepted result, as the ticket first put it: a `blocked` or `failed` run would drop the hold without asking, and the restart's run could end `done`. Keeping only the latest reason: the refused `done` that parks the told run says nothing of the choices, so the restart's run would be told to ask choices it is not shown. Writing a clear as soon as the asking run ended: a round whose post failed would leave the ref with neither the hold nor the questions. Keeping refusals of decisions only: the ticket asks for the reason too, and a refusal of any kind told across commands is never freed twice.
